On semi-classical limit of spatially homogeneous quantum Boltzmann equation: weak convergence
Ling-Bing He, Xuguang Lu, Mario Pulvirenti

Abstract
. It is expected in physics that the homogeneous quantum Boltzmann equation with Fermi-Dirac or Bose-Einstein statistics and with Maxwell-Boltzmann operator (neglecting effect of the statistics) for the weak coupled gases will converge to the homogeneous Fokker-Planck-Landau equation as the Planck constant ℏ \hbar tends to zero. In this paper and the upcoming work [ 17 ] , we will provide a mathematical justification on this semi-classical limit. Key ingredients into the proofs are the new framework to catch the weak projection gradient , which is motivated by Villani [ 28 ] to identify the H H -solutions for Fokker-Planck-Landau equation, and the symmetric structure inside the cubic terms of the collision operators. Keywords: Quantum Boltzmann equation, Fokker-Planck-Landau equation, Semi-classical limit.



1.
Introduction

The quantum Boltzmann equations for Fermi-Dirac and
Bose-Einstein statistics were proposed
by Uehling and Uhlembeck in
[
27
]
(after Nordheim
[
23
]
) on the basis of heuristic arguments. In contrast with the usual classical Boltzmann equation valid for a rarefied gas in the so called Boltzmann-Grad limit, such equations should be derived from the evolution of real Fermion and Bosons in the so called weak-coupling limit (see
[
3
]
and
[
5
]
). From a physical point of view,
it is expected that the semi-classical limit of the quantum Boltzmann equations is the Fokker-Planck-Landau (FPL) equation.
This is mainly because that from the point of view of particle system, FPL equation is the effective equation associated with a dense and weakly interacting gas of classical particles (see
[
24
]
,
[
10
]
).
Therefore it is not surprising that the semi-classical limits of the solutions of quantum Boltzmann equations is expected to be solutions of the FPL equation.

It should be stressed that in the weak-coupling limit, the kinetic picture for quantum systems yields a Boltzmann type evolution, basically a jump process, and not a diffusion in velocity as for classical systems. This is because of the tunnel effect which does assign a finite probability to a test particle to go freely in the scattering process. However when
\hbar
goes to zero, this effect is negligible and the diffusion is recovered in the limit, provided that the diffusion coefficient

M_{\widehat{\phi}}
(defined in (
1.11
) below) is finite.

The aim of the present work and the forthcoming work
[
17
]
is to provide a mathematical
justification for such semi-classical limit in the so-called homogeneous case.

1.1.
Setting of the problem

In this subsection, we will first introduce the quantum Boltzmann equation and Fokker-Planck-Landau equation and then list some basic properties of the solutions.

1.1.1.
Introduction of equations

The spatially homogeneous quantum Boltzmann equation reads

(1.1)

{\partial}_{t}f(t,{\bf v})=Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)(t,{\bf v}),\qquad(t,{\bf v})\in(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}

where

\varepsilon:=2\pi\hbar
,
\hbar>0
is the Planck constant,

Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)(t,{\bf v})=Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f(t,\cdot))({\bf v})

and

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)({\bf v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Big(f^{\prime}f_{*}^{\prime}(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f)(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f_{*})-ff_{*}(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f^{\prime})(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f_{*}^{\prime})\Big){\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}.

Several comments on the collision operator
Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
are in order:

(1)

\lambda\in\{0,-1,+1\}

(2)

We use standard shorthand notations
f=f({\bf v}),\,f_{*}=f({\bf v}_{*}),\,f^{\prime}=f({\bf v}^{\prime}),\,f_{*}^{\prime}=f({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}),
where
({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})
and
({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})
are the velocities of particles before and after their collision, and
{\bf v}^{\prime}
,
{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}
are given by the
\omega
-representation

(1.2)

\displaystyle{\bf v}^{\prime}={\bf v}-(({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega)\omega,\quad{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}={\bf v}_{*}+(({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega)\omega,\quad{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3},\quad\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2},

(3)

B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
is defined by (see e.g.
[
5
]
)

\displaystyle B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{|({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\bigg\{\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}+\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\bigg\}\qquad{\rm if}\quad\lambda=0\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{|({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\bigg(\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)+\lambda\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)\bigg)^{2}\qquad{\rm if}\quad\lambda=\pm 1\end{array}\right.

(4)

\widehat{\phi}(r):=\widehat{\phi(|\cdot|)}(\xi)
with
|\xi|=r
, where
the function
\widehat{\phi(|\cdot|)}(\xi)
is the Fourier transform of the particle interaction potential

{\bf x}\mapsto\phi(|{\bf x}|)\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

In particular, we specify that

\bullet
if
\lambda=0
, (
1.1
) corresponds to the spatially homogeneous Maxwell-Boltzmann equation (MB) with a semi-classical collision kernel:

[formula]

{\partial}_{t}f(t,{\bf v})=Q^{\varepsilon}_{0}(f)(t,{\bf v}),\quad(t,{\bf v})\in(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}.

\bullet
if
\lambda=-1
, (
1.1
) corresponds to the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation for Fermi-Dirac particles (FD):

[formula]

{\partial}_{t}f(t,{\bf v})=Q_{-1}^{\varepsilon}(f)(t,{\bf v}),\quad(t,{\bf v})\in(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}.

\bullet
if
\lambda=1
, (
1.1
) corresponds to the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation for Bose-Einstein particles (BE):

[formula]

{\partial}_{t}f(t,{\bf v})=Q_{+1}^{\varepsilon}(f)(t,{\bf v}),\quad(t,{\bf v})\in(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}.

Remark 1.1
.

In the present paper we call MB collision operator the one obtained from the FD or BE operators by neglecting the cubic terms (i.e. the case
\lambda=0
). We note that for any suitable (e.g. integrable or nonnegative) Borel measurable function
\Psi
on
{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}
satisfying

\Psi({\bf w},{\bf w}_{*},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\Psi({\bf w}_{*},{\bf w},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})
, applying the formula (
1.25
) twice
with the reflection
\sigma\to-\sigma
in between, one has the identity:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}|({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega|\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}|({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega|\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\omega.

This implies that the kernel
B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
in the collision operator
Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)({\bf v})
can also be replaced by

(1.8)

\widetilde{B}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)=\frac{|({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cdot\omega|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\bigg\{2\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}+\lambda 2\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)\bigg\}

and thus for the case
\lambda=0
we recover the MB cross section in
[
4
]
.
According to
[
6
]
and
[
7
]
, the case
\lambda=0
is just the cross section
arising from the weak coupling limit of quantum particles without statistics.
For the case
\lambda=\pm 1
, the kernel
B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)

is still nonnegative while
\widetilde{B}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
in (
1.8
)
is not, but
the second term in the righthand side of (
1.8
) is expected to vanish in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
because it is the product of two terms which are going to concentrate in different points. Therefore the effect of the statistics should be negligible in this limit, up to the control of the cubic terms.
We also remark that the cross-section entering in the quantum Boltzmann equation is the one obtained in the Born approximation, because of the weakness of the potential.

The Fokker-Planck-Landau(FPL) equation was originally obtained by Landau from classical Boltzmann equation with cutoff Rutherford cross section (see
[
19
]
).
Mathematically the spatially homogeneous Fokker-Planck-Landau equation
associated to the Coulomb potential reads

[formula]

{\partial}_{t}f(t,{\bf v})=Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}),\quad(t,{\bf v})\in(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}

where
Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v})=Q_{L}(f(t,\cdot))({\bf v})
,

(1.9)

\displaystyle Q_{L}(f)({\bf v})=M_{\widehat{\phi}}\nabla_{{\bf v}}\cdot\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{1}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\big(\nabla_{{\bf v}}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}}\big)f({\bf v})f({\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}{\bf v}_{*},

(1.10)

\displaystyle M_{\widehat{\phi}}\,\,{\rm is\,\,defined\,\,in\,\eqref{ker2}},\quad\Pi({\bf z})={\rm I}-{\bf n}\otimes{\bf n}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3\times 3},\quad{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|\,\,{\rm for}\,\,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},

\displaystyle{\rm I}=(\delta_{ij})_{3\times 3},\,\,{\rm the\,\,unit\,\,matrix},\quad{\bf n}\otimes{\bf n}={\bf n}{\bf n}^{\tau}=(n_{i}n_{j})_{3\times 3}\quad{\rm for}\,\,{\bf n}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})^{\tau}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3},

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bf v}}=({\partial}_{v_{1}},{\partial}_{v_{2}},{\partial}_{v_{3}})^{\tau}.

Remark 1.2
.

The word
Coulomb
usually used for the FPL equation with the kernel singularity

\frac{1}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}
is somehow misleading. Such a singularity has nothing to do with the Coulomb interaction. Indeed the FPL equation with different diffusive coefficient
M_{\widehat{\phi}}
can also be derived even though the underlying particle system evolves under the action of a two-body smooth potential. For Coulomb potential, we have
\frac{1}{2\pi}M_{\widehat{\phi}}=\log(\Lambda),
which is called Coulomb logarithm.

In
[
4
]
, Benedetto and Pulvirenti proved the
operator convergence
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)=Q_{L}(f)
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
for every
\lambda\in\{0,-1,+1\}
and for a suitable class of integrable functions
f
, i.e.

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(f)({\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{L}(f)({\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}\quad\forall\,\psi\in\mathcal{S}

where
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
is the class of Schwartz functions.

In this paper, we prove a further result: roughly speaking,
solutions (strong, mild or weak) of Eq.(MB), Eq.(FD), and Eq.(BE)
converge weakly to certain weak solutions of Eq.(FPL) as (up to subsequences)
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty).
We do these under the following assumptions (
1.11
)-(
1.12
).

Basic assumptions on the Fourier transform of the interaction potential:

(\mathbf{A1})
.

For simplicity, we assume that the diffusive coefficient

M_{\widehat{\phi}}
in (
1.9
)
is normalized, i.e.

(1.11)

M_{\widehat{\phi}}:=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{3}|\widehat{\phi}(r)|^{2}{\rm d}r=1.

(\mathbf{A2})
.

In order to study the convergence from Eq.(FD) to Eq.(FPL) and from Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL), we further assume that

(1.12)

\widehat{\phi}\in C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}),\quad A_{\widehat{\phi}}:=\sup_{r\geq 0}r|\widehat{\phi}(r)|<\infty.

It should be noted that Proposition
6.2
in the Appendix shows that
if there exists
r_{0}\geq 0
such that

(1.13)

r\mapsto r\widehat{\phi}(r)\,\,\,{\rm is\,\,\,monotone\,\,\,in}\,\,\,(r_{0},\infty)

then the function
{\bf x}\mapsto\phi(|{\bf x}|)
defined by

(1.14)

\phi(\rho)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}\rho}\int_{0}^{R}r\widehat{\phi}(r)\sin(\rho r){\rm d}r,\quad\rho>0

is the corresponding interaction potential. Furthermore if
r_{0}=0
and
\widehat{\phi}(r)\geq 0
in

(0,\infty)
, then
\phi(\rho)\geq 0
in

(0,\infty).

1.1.2.
Basic properties of the equations

First of all we note that if
f
is a solution of
Eq.(
1.1
)
or Eq.(FPL), then it enjoys the conservation of mass, momentum and the energy, i.e.

(1.15)

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\,1\\
\,{\bf v}\\
\,|{\bf v}|^{2}\end{array}\right)f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\,1\\
\,{\bf v}\\
\,|{\bf v}|^{2}\end{array}\right)f(0,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}\qquad\forall\,t\geq 0.

Now let us introduce the entropy and the famous
H
-theorem
1
1

1

In this paper, “the
H
-theorem” means that
the solution
f
satisfies either the entropy identity like (
1.19
) or the entropy inequality like (
1.29
).
for Eq.(
1.1
) and
Eq.(FPL).

\bullet
For MB model (
\lambda=0
) and FPL model, the corresponding entropies

H_{0}(f)
and
H_{L}(f)
are given by

\displaystyle H_{0}(f)=H_{L}(f)=H(f):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f({\bf v})\log f({\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}.

\bullet
For FD model and BE model (i.e.
\lambda\in\{-1,+1\}
), the entropies are defined by

\displaystyle H_{\lambda\varepsilon^{3}}(f):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\Big(f\log f-\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon^{3}}(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f)\log(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f)\Big){\rm d}{\bf v},\quad\lambda\in\{-1,+1\}.

Let
f^{\varepsilon}
be a solution of Eq.(MB), Eq.(FD), Eq.(BE) (i.e.
\lambda=0,-1,+1
) respectively
with the initial datum
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
that belongs to

L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,
L^{1}_{2}\cap L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
(with
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
), and

L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
respectively.
Then, by formal calculation, the
H
-theorem for
f^{\varepsilon}
can be stated as follows:

(1.16)

H_{\lambda\varepsilon^{3}}(f^{\varepsilon}(t))+\int_{0}^{t}D^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(f^{\varepsilon}(s)){\rm d}s=H_{\lambda\varepsilon^{3}}(f^{\varepsilon}_{0})\qquad\forall\,t\geq 0

where

(1.17)

\displaystyle D^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Gamma\Big(f^{\prime}f_{*}^{\prime}(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f)(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f_{*}),\,ff_{*}(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f^{\prime})(1+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}f_{*}^{\prime})\Big){\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*},

(1.18)

\Gamma(a,b)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle(a-b)\log(\frac{a}{b}),\quad\qquad{\rm if}\quad a,b>0;\\
\displaystyle\,\,\infty,\,\,\,\,\qquad\qquad\quad\qquad{\rm if}\quad a=0<b\,\,\,{\rm or}\,\,\,b=0<a;\\
\displaystyle\,\,\,0,\,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\rm if}\quad a=b=0\,.\end{array}\right.

Let
f
be a solution of Eq.(FPL)
with the initial datum
f_{0}
that belongs to

L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.
By formal calculation, the
H
-theorem for solutions of Eq.(FPL) can be stated as follows:

(1.19)

H(f(t))+\int_{0}^{t}D_{L}(f(s)){\rm d}s=H(f_{0})\qquad\forall\,t\geq 0

with

(1.20)

D_{L}(f)=2\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\frac{1}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\big|\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})(\nabla_{\bf v}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}})\sqrt{f({\bf v})f({\bf v}_{*})}\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}.

Here and below we denote as usual for functionals of
{\bf v}\mapsto f(t,{\bf v})
as

H(f(t))=H(f(t,\cdot)),\quad D_{L}(f(t))=D_{L}(f(t,\cdot)),\quad\|f(t)\|=\|f(t,\cdot)\|,\quad{\rm etc.}

Several remarks are in order:

Remark 1.3
.

According to the physical meaning
of the Fermi-Dirac model, the factor
1-\varepsilon^{3}f(t,{\bf v})
should be nonnegative, i.e.

0\leq f(t,{\bf v})\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
.
It can be proved
that if initially
0\leq f_{0}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
, then
0\leq f(t,\cdot)\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
for all
t\geq 0
. Thus the entropy
H_{-\varepsilon^{3}}(f(t))
for Eq.(FD) is always finite for all
t\geq 0
.

Remark 1.4
.

The entropy
H(f)
for Eq.(MB) and Eq.(FPL), and the entropy
H_{+\varepsilon^{3}}(f)
for Eq.(BE) are all
finite if initially they are finite.

Remark 1.5
.

In our proof, the
H
-theorem is very useful for the convergence from
Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FPL) and from Eq.(FD) to Eq.(FPL). But we have no idea how to apply it
to prove the convergence from Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL) because the
H
-theorem for Eq.(BE) does not
provide the
L^{1}
weak compactness of solutions.

1.2.
Basic notations

Let us first introduce some function spaces and norms which will be used throughout the paper:

\bullet

L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})=L^{1}_{0}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

L^{1}_{k}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})=\Big\{f\in L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})\,\,\big|\,\,\|f\|_{L^{1}_{k}}:=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}(1+|{\bf v}|^{2})^{k/2}|f({\bf v})|{\rm d}{\bf v}<\infty\Big\},\,\,k\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
;

\bullet

L^{1}_{k}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})=\Big\{0\leq f\in L^{1}_{k}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\,\,\big|\,\,\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}f({\bf v})|\log f({\bf v})|{\rm d}{\bf v}<\infty\Big\}
;

\bullet

L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{k}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))=\Big\{f\,\,{\rm Lebesgue\,\,measurable\,\,on}\,\,[0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\,\,\big|\,\,\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{k}}<\infty\Big\}
;

\bullet

L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{k}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))=\Big\{0\leq f\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{k}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))\,\big|\,\,\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}\|f(t)\log f(t)\|_{L^{1}}<\infty\Big\};

\bullet

\|f\|_{\infty}=\sup\limits_{{\bf x}\in E}|f({\bf x})|
if
f
is bounded on its domain of definition
E
.

1.3.
Main results, difficulties and strategies of the problem

In this subsection, we will give the precise statement for our main results, explain the difficulties of the proof for the weak convergence, and sketch our strategy of the proof.

1.3.1.
From Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FPL)

Our goal is to prove that,
starting from Eq.(MB), the weak solutions of Eq.(MB) (up to subsequences) converge to an
H-solution
of Eq.(FPL). Before addressing the difficulties and the new ideas for this problem, we begin with some definitions on weak solutions of associated equations.

Definition 1.6
.

Given
\varepsilon=2\pi\hbar>0
.
Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega),D^{\varepsilon}_{0}(f)
be given by (
(3)
),(
1.11
),(
1.17
) with
\lambda=0
.
We say that a function
f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))

is a weak solution of Eq.(MB) if

f^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the following
(i),(ii)
:

(i)

f^{\varepsilon}
conserves the mass, momentum, and energy, and satisfies the entropy inequality

(1.21)

H(f^{\varepsilon}(t))+\int_{0}^{t}D^{\varepsilon}_{0}(f^{\varepsilon}(s)){\rm d}s\leq H(f^{\varepsilon}_{0}),\quad t\geq 0.

(ii)
For any
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

t\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f^{\varepsilon}(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

is absolutely continuous on

[0,\infty)
and the differential equality
2
2

2

In this paper we say that a function
g(t)
is
is absolutely continuous on

[0,\infty)
if
g
is absolutely
continuous on every bounded interval
[a,b]\subset[0,\infty)
. Recalling that
this is equivalent to that
\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}g\in L^{1}_{loc}([0,\infty))
and

g(b)-g(a)=\int_{a}^{b}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}g(t){\rm d}t
for all

0\leq a<b<\infty
.

(1.22)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f^{\varepsilon}(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}B_{0}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\big({f^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}{f_{*}^{\varepsilon}}^{\prime}-{f^{\varepsilon}}{f_{*}^{\varepsilon}}\big)\big(\psi+\psi_{*}-\psi^{\prime}-\psi_{*}^{\prime}\big){\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}{\rm d}\omega

holds for almost every
t\in(0,\infty)
.

Remark 1.7
.

The weak solutions of Eq.(MB) has been
considered in
[
14
]
for the so-called very soft potentials that includes our MB model because

(1.23)

\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B_{0}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\sin^{2}(\theta)\cos^{2}(\theta)\big|_{\theta=\arccos({\bf n}\cdot\omega)}{\rm d}\omega\leq\frac{4}{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{3}}

which comes from the assumption (
1.11
). Here
{\bf n}=({\bf v-v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|

and throughout this paper we define
{\bf n}=(1,0,0)^{\tau}
for
{\bf v-v}_{*}={\bf 0}
. Note that as in
[
14
]
, the
\sigma
-representation
will be used:

(1.24)

{\bf v}^{\prime}=\frac{{\bf v+v}_{*}}{2}+\frac{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\quad{\bf v}^{\prime}_{*}=\frac{{\bf v+v}_{*}}{2}-\frac{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\quad\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}.

The
\omega
-representation (
1.2
) and the
\sigma
-representation (
1.24
)
have the following relation (see e.g. section 4 of chapter 1 in
[
29
]
):

(1.25)

\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}|({\bf v-v}_{*})\cdot\omega|\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})\big|_{\omega-{\rm rep.}}{\rm d}\omega=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}|{\bf v-v}_{*}|\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})\big|_{\sigma-{\rm rep.}}{\rm d}\sigma.

From this, it is easy to see that the inequality (
1.23
)
(with the
\omega
-representation) is just the inequality (1.6) in
[
14
]
(with the
\sigma
-representation) with
N=3,\gamma=-3
, and a different constant. Therefore by entropy control,
the righthand side of (
1.22
) is absolutely convergent (see also (
3.5
) below).

The following is a definition of
H
-solutions in spirit of Villani
[
28
]
.

Definition 1.8
.

We say that a function
f\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))

is an
H
-solution of Eq.(FPL) if

f
satisfies the following
(i),(ii), (iii)
:

(i)

f
conserves the mass, momentum, and energy.

(ii)
The function
(t,{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\mapsto\sqrt{f(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}
has
the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{f(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}
in
{\bf v-v_{*}}\neq{\bf 0}
defined in Definition
6.6
in Appendix, and

\int_{0}^{\infty}D_{L}(f(t)){\rm d}t<\infty

where

(1.26)

D_{L}(f(t)):=2\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\big|\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{f(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}.

(iii)
For any
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

t\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

is absolutely continuous on
[0,\infty)
and the differential equality

(1.27)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

holds for almost every
t\in[0,\infty)
, where the righthand side of
(
1.27
) is defined by

(1.28)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\frac{\nabla\psi({\bf v})-\nabla\psi({\bf v}_{*})}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\cdot\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\big(f(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}_{*})\big)\,{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}.\qquad

Besides, if an H-solution
f
also satisfies

(1.29)

H(f(t))+\int_{0}^{t}D_{L}(f(s)){\rm d}s\leq H(f(0))\qquad\forall\,t\geq 0

then we say that
f
satisfies the entropy inequality.

Some remarks are in order:

Remark 1.9
.

The weak projection gradient operator
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}
is introduced in Definition
6.6
in Appendix. We emphasize that
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}F({\bf v,v}_{*})
does not equal
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})(\nabla_{\bf v}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}})F({\bf v,v}_{*})
unless the usual gradient operators
\nabla_{\bf v}
and
\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}}
are both well-defined for

F({\bf v,v}_{*})
.

Remark 1.10
.

The assumption (ii) in Definition
1.8
and Lemma
6.8
(b), Lemma
6.9
in Appendix imply that

\sqrt{ff_{*}}
and
ff_{*}

also have weak projection gradients
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}}
and

\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}(ff_{*})
in
{\bf v-v}_{*}\neq{\bf 0}
, and they have the following relations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}}\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}}=\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}(ff_{*})=2\sqrt{ff_{*}}\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}}\,.

Since

|\nabla\psi({\bf v})-\nabla\psi({\bf v}_{*})|\leq\|D^{2}\psi\|_{\infty}|{\bf v-v}_{*}|
, it follows
from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that for almost all
t\in(0,\infty)
the integral in the righthand side of
(
1.28
) is absolutely convergent and

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\frac{|\nabla\psi({\bf v})-\nabla\psi({\bf v}_{*})|}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\sqrt{ff_{*}|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\,\big|\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}\big|\,{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f_{0}}\|D^{2}\psi\|_{\infty}\sqrt{D_{L}(f(t))}

where the constant
C_{f_{0}}<\infty
depends only on the mass and energy of
f_{0}
.

Remark 1.11
.

Recall that the usual weak form of Eq.(FPL) is defined by

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}L[\psi]({\bf v,v}_{*})f(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*},\quad t\in(0,\infty)

for all
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, where

(1.30)

L[\psi]({\bf v,v}_{*})=\frac{1}{2|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}(\nabla_{{\bf v}}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}})\cdot\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\big(\nabla\psi({\bf v})-\nabla\psi({\bf v}_{*})\big).

The existence of weak solutions of Eq.(FPL) has been a problem because

L[\psi]({\bf v,v}_{*})=O(\frac{1}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|})
and
ff_{*}/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|
may be non-integrable on
{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}
. This is the main motivation in
[
28
]
to introduce the
H-solution
to Eq.(FPL).

To prove the convergence from the weak solution of Eq.(MB) to
H
-solution of Eq.(FPL), the main difficulty lies in the derivation of the existence of
weak projection gradient
of solutions of Eq.(PFL) by taking the weak limit
with respect to
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Note that the Maxwell-Boltzmann kernel
B_{0}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
in Eq.(MB) with the assumption
(
1.11
) can not be written as the product form
b(\cos\theta)\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)
used in
[
28
]
, and this is one reason that we cannot directly follow the proof used there. We will instead establish a framework to deal with the
weak projection gradient
and this can be regarded as one of our contributions in this paper:

\bullet
We introduce
new types of test function spaces to define the weak projection gradient which are the keys to identify the
H
-solution for Fokker-Planck-Landau equation. This is motivated by Villani
[
28
]
. In this way, we clarify that generally,
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\neq\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})(\nabla_{{\bf v}}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}})
.

\bullet
We introduce tools such as the change of variables(see Section
2.1
) and a suitable measure space (see (
3.15
)) to prove the existence of weak projection gradients
and to derive the integrability of
D_{L}(f(t))
from
D_{0}^{\varepsilon}(f^{\varepsilon}(t))
in the new framework.

Our first main result of the paper is as follows:

Theorem 1.12
.

[From Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FPL)]
Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
) and (
1.11
) with
\lambda=0
.
Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0,f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})
satisfy

(1.31)

\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v})(1+|{\bf v}|^{2}+|\log f^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v})|){\rm d}{\bf v}<\infty.

Let

f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}))
be weak solutions of the Eq.(
MB
)
with the initial data
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
. Then for any sequence

{\cal E}=\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
satisfying
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)
,
there exist a subsequence, still denote it as

{\cal E}
, and an
H
-solution
f
of Eq.(
FPL
),
such that
f^{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)\rightharpoonup f(t,\cdot)
weakly in
L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
(as
{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0
)
for every
t\in[0,\infty)
. More precisely we have

(1.32)

\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})f^{\varepsilon}(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}\quad\forall\,\psi\in L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\quad\forall\,t\in[0,\infty)

and

(1.33)

\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}{\rm d}t\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{0}^{\varepsilon}(f^{\varepsilon})(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{0}^{T}{\rm d}t\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf v})Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

for all
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
and all

T\in(0,\infty)
, where the righthand side of (
1.33
) is defined by (
1.28
).
Besides, it also holds

(1.34)

H(f(t))+\int_{0}^{t}D_{L}(f(s)){\rm d}s\leq\liminf_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}H(f^{\varepsilon}_{0})\qquad\forall\,t>0.

In particular, if in addition that

|f_{0}^{\varepsilon}-f_{0}|\big(1+\log^{+}(|f_{0}^{\varepsilon}-f_{0}|)\big)\to 0
in
L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
as

\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
for some
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
(for instance

f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\equiv f_{0}
), then this

H
-solution
f
with the initial datum
f(0,\cdot)=f_{0}
also satisfies the entropy inequality (
1.29
).

Remark 1.13
.

This result indicates that starting from any smooth short-range potential function
\phi
, the Maxwell-Boltzmann equation will converge to the Fokker-Planck-Landau equation in the weak coupling limit, which is expected in physics and is consistent with Remark
1.1
. Moreover comparing to the grazing collisions limit proposed in
[
28
]
, the weak coupling limit enables us to get the right diffusion coefficient
M_{\widehat{\phi}}
for the resulting equation.

Remark 1.14
.

As emphasized above, here we have only proved that the function
\sqrt{ff_{*}/|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|}
has the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}\sqrt{ff_{*}/|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|}
in
{\bf v-v}_{*}\neq{\bf 0}
, and this does not directly imply
the existence of the usual weak gradient
\nabla_{{\bf v}}\sqrt{f(t,{\bf v})}
. Thus the result in
[
16
]

cannot be directly applied to derive the weighted
L^{p}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\,(p>1)
estimate for
H
-solutions, and so it remains unclear whether an
H
-solution can be a usual weak solution.

1.3.2.
From Eq.(FD) or Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL)

Let us first introduce the definition of weak solutions of Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE).

Definition 1.15
.

Given
\varepsilon=2\pi\hbar>0
.
Let
B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
) with
\lambda=\pm 1
.
We say that a function
0\leq f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))

is a weak solution of Eq.(FD) (
\lambda=-1
)
and Eq.(BE) (
\lambda=+1
) respectively if

f^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the following
(i),(ii)
and for the case
\lambda=-1
assuming further that

f^{\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
on
[0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
:

(i)

f^{\varepsilon}
conserves the mass, momentum, and energy.

(ii)
For any
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

t\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi({\bf v})f^{\varepsilon}(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

is absolutely continuous on

[0,\infty)
and the differential equality

(1.35)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi f^{\varepsilon}{\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]f^{\varepsilon}f^{\varepsilon}_{*}{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}+R_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\psi,f^{\varepsilon}(t)]

holds for almost every
t\in(0,\infty)
, where

(1.36)

\displaystyle L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\big(\psi^{\prime}+\psi_{*}^{\prime}-\psi-\psi_{*}\big){\rm d}\omega,

(1.37)

\displaystyle R_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\psi,f]=\lambda\varepsilon^{3}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\big(\psi^{\prime}+\psi_{*}^{\prime}-\psi-\psi_{*}\big)f^{\prime}ff_{*}{\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}.

Remark 1.16
.

Notice that the assumption (
1.12
) with
definition (
(3)
) implies that

B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{3}}A_{\widehat{\phi}}\|\widehat{\phi}\|_{\infty}(1+\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\theta)})
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega){\rm d}\omega\leq\frac{16\pi}{\varepsilon^{3}}A_{\widehat{\phi}}\|\widehat{\phi}\|_{\infty}<\infty

where
\theta=\arccos(|{\bf n}\cdot\omega|)
,
{\bf n}=({\bf v-v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|
. This ensures that
the integrals in the righthand side of (
1.35
) are absolutely convergent for
the case
\lambda=-1
and thus the weak form (
1.35
) is rigorous. For
\lambda=+1
, however, the weak form (
1.35
) maybe holds rigorously only for short time interval (see e.g.
[
12
]
for local in time existence) or holds only formally for large time interval because (due to the effect of velocity concentration) there has been no results
on the global in time existence of weak solutions
f^{\varepsilon}
in

L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))
for general initial data. So far most available general results for
\lambda=+1
have been only proven for isotropic measure-valued weak solutions (see below).

In the study of the convergence from Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL), the most difficult problem is how to prove the zero limit of the cubic term:

(1.38)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{T}R_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\psi,f^{\varepsilon}(t)]{\rm d}t=0\qquad\forall\,T\in(0,\infty),\,\,\,\forall\,\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

Indeed, from the analysis in Section 2 and Section 4 one can see that it is very
different from the isotropic case that
for anisotropic weak solutions
f^{\varepsilon}
of Eq.(FD) or Eq.(BE),
it is even hard to prove the
L^{\infty}
-boundedness of
\{R_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\psi,f^{\varepsilon}(t)]\,\,|\,\,\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}],t\in[0,T]\}
.
So far we can only prove the zero limit (
1.38
) for
isotropic solutions because in the isotropic case there are more symmetric and cancellation properties
in the cubic terms (see Step 3 in the proof of Lemma
4.3
).
By the way, if the zero limit (
1.38
) can be proven to hold for
general weak solutions
f^{\varepsilon}
of Eq.(FD), then such a model convergence to Eq.(FPL) maybe holds also (for instance) for the quantum Boltzmann equation for Haldane exclusion statistics (see e.g.
[
8
]
,
[
2
]
), provided that the corresponding
collision kernel has a similar structure as those obtained from the weak-coupling limit.
These are of course completely new and more involved.

Now let us introduce definitions about isotropic solutions.

Definition 1.17
.

If a solution
f

is a function of
(t,|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
only,
then we say that
f
is an isotropic solution (or radially symmetric solution).

For convenience of analysis we will write an isotropic solution as

f=f(t,|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)=f(t,x)
with
x=|{\bf v}|^{2}/2
, and
the measure element

f(t,|{\bf v}|^{2}/2){\rm d}{\bf v}

will be replaced by
4\pi\sqrt{2}f(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x
. The corresponding
test functions are chosen from
C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
.

Case 1
: From Eq.(FD) to Eq.(FPL).

It is easily calculated that the isotropic form of Eq.(FPL) is

(1.39)

{\partial}_{t}f(t,x)=\frac{16\pi}{3\sqrt{x}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\big(f(t,y){\partial}_{x}f(t,x)-f(t,x){\partial}_{y}f(t,y)\big)(x\wedge y)^{3/2}{\rm d}y,\quad(t,x)\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{>0}^{2}.

Here and below

a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\},\quad a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}.

Definition 1.18
.

[Isotropic weak solutions of Eq.(FPL)]

A function

0\leq f\in L^{\infty}\big([0,\infty),L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},(1+x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x)\big)

is called an isotropic weak solution of Eq.(FPL) (or equivalently a weak solution of Eq.(
1.39
)) if

(i)

f
conserves the mass and energy:

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x)f(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x\equiv\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x)f(0,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x,\,t\geq 0.

(ii)
For any

\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
, it holds

(1.40)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x)f(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}L[\varphi](x,y)f(t,x)f(t,y)\sqrt{x}\sqrt{y}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}y,\quad t\in[0,\infty)

where

(1.41)

L[\varphi](x,y)=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{x\vee y}}\Big(\frac{2}{3}\big(\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)+\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)\big)(x\wedge y)-\big(\varphi^{\prime}(x)-\varphi^{\prime}(y)\big){\rm sgn}(x-y)\Big).

Remark 1.19
.

Setting
L[\varphi](0,0)=0
, the function
(x,y)\mapsto L[\varphi](x,y)

is continuous in
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
and

(1.42)

|L[\varphi](x,y)|\leq C_{0}\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}),\quad(x,y)\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}

where
\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}=\sup\limits_{x\geq 0}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)|
and
C_{0}\in(0,\infty)
denotes an absolute constant.

We also need to define the isotropic weak solutions for Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE) (see
[
22
]
and Appendix in
[
13
]
). We first introduce, for any
given
\Phi\in C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2})
, a function
W_{\Phi}(x,y,z)
as follows:

(1.43)

W_{\Phi}(x,y,z):=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{xyz}}\int_{|\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y}|\vee|\sqrt{x_{*}}-\sqrt{z}|}^{(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})\wedge(\sqrt{x_{*}}+\sqrt{z})}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi(\sqrt{2}s,\sqrt{2}Y_{*}){\rm d}\theta\quad{\rm if}\quad x_{*},x,y,z>0,

(1.44)

W_{\Phi}(x,y,z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{(4\pi)^{2}}{\sqrt{yz}}\Phi\big(\sqrt{2y},\sqrt{2z}\,\big)\qquad\,\,\qquad\qquad{\rm if}\quad x=0,\,y>0,\,z>0\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{(4\pi)^{2}}{\sqrt{xz}}\Phi\big(\sqrt{2x},\sqrt{2(z-x)}\,\big)\qquad\qquad{\rm if}\quad y=0,\,z>x>0\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{(4\pi)^{2}}{\sqrt{xy}}\Phi\big(\sqrt{2(y-x)},\sqrt{2x}\,\big)\qquad\quad\quad{\rm if}\quad z=0,\,y>x>0,\\
\\
\displaystyle 0\qquad\quad\qquad{\rm others}\end{array}\right.

(1.45)

Y_{*}:=Y_{*}(x,y,z,s,\theta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{\Big(z-\frac{(x-y+s^{2})^{2}}{4s^{2}}\Big)_{+}}+e^{{\rm i}\theta}\sqrt{\Big(x-\frac{(x-y+s^{2})^{2}}{4s^{2}}\Big)_{+}}\,\right|\quad{\rm if}\quad s>0\\
\\
\displaystyle 0\qquad\qquad{\rm if}\quad s=0\end{array}\right.

for all
x,y,z\geq 0
, where
(u)_{+}=\max\{u,0\},

{\rm i}=\sqrt{-1}
.

Definition 1.20
.

[Isotropic weak solutions of Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE)]

A function
0\leq f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,\infty),L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},\\
(1+x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x)\big)

is called an isotropic weak solution of Eq.(FD)(
\lambda=-1
) or Eq.(BE)(
\lambda=+1
) if

(i)

f^{\varepsilon}
conserves the mass and energy:

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x)f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x)f^{\varepsilon}(0,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x
for all
t\geq 0
, and for the case
\lambda=-1
it also requires that
f^{\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
on
[0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
.

(ii)
For any

\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
, it holds

(1.46)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x)f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}{\cal J}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](y,z)f^{\varepsilon}(t,y)f^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\sqrt{yz}{\rm d}y{\rm d}z

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda\varepsilon^{3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{3}}{\cal K}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z)f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)f^{\varepsilon}(t,y)f^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\sqrt{xyz}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y{\rm d}z\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,\infty)

where

(1.47)

{\cal J}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](y,z)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{y+z}{\cal K}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x,

(1.48)

{\cal K}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z)=W_{\Phi_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}}(x,y,z)\big(\varphi(x)+\varphi(x_{*})-\varphi(y)-\varphi(z)\big),

x,y,z\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
,
x_{*}=(y+z-x)_{+}
, and

(1.49)

\Phi_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(r,\rho)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\Big(\widehat{\phi}\big(\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\big)+\lambda\widehat{\phi}\big(\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}\big)\Big)^{2}\quad with\quad\widehat{\phi}\in C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}),\quad\lambda\in\{-1,+1\}.

Remark 1.21
.

Thanks to the identity

(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})\wedge(\sqrt{x_{*}}+\sqrt{z})-|\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y}|\vee|\sqrt{x_{*}}-\sqrt{z}|=2\min\{\sqrt{x},\sqrt{x_{*}},\sqrt{y},\sqrt{z}\,\},

we have

\displaystyle\big|\varphi(x)+\varphi(x_{*})-\varphi(y)-\varphi(z)\big|\leq\min\Big\{4\|\varphi\|_{\infty},\,2\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}(|x-y|\wedge|x-z|),\,\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}|x-y||x-z|\Big\}.

From this together with
\Phi_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\in C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2})
, it is easily seen that for any
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
,
the function
(y,z)\mapsto(1+\sqrt{y}+\sqrt{z})^{-1}{\cal J}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](y,z)

belongs to
C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2})
and
(x,y,z)\mapsto{\cal K}_{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z)

belongs to
C_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{3})
. These ensure that the integrals in the righthand side of
Eq.(
1.46
) are absolutely convergent for all
0\leq f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,\infty),L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},(1+x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x)\big)
.

In this paper, for any sequence
{\cal E}=\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
satisfying
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)
, we assume without loss of generality that

\varepsilon_{n}
is strictly decreasing, so that the sequence
{\cal E}
can be treated as subset of

(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, and thus “
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)
” and
“
{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0
” have the same meaning, so that we can denote

(1.50)

\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}a_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{\varepsilon_{n}},\quad\liminf_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}a_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\inf_{n\geq k}a_{\varepsilon_{n}},\quad\limsup_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}a_{\varepsilon}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{n\geq k}a_{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Theorem 1.22
.

[From Eq.(FD) to Eq.(FPL)]
Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{-1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
)
with
\lambda=-1
.
Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0,0\leq f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},(1+x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x)\cap L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
satisfy

(1.51)

f^{\varepsilon}_{0}\leq\varepsilon^{-3},\quad\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}f^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)(1+x+|\log f^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)|)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x<\infty.

Let

f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
with the initial data
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x)
be isotropic weak solutions of the Eq.(
FD
)
satisfying the entropy equality (
1.16
) with
\lambda=-1
and

f^{\varepsilon}=f^{\varepsilon}(t,|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
. Then for any sequence

{\cal E}=\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
satisfying
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)
,
there exist a subsequence, still denote it as

{\cal E}
, and an isotropic weak solution

f\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},(1+x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x))
of Eq.(
FPL
),
such that
f^{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)\rightharpoonup f(t,\cdot)
weakly in
L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0},\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x)

(as
{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0
) for every
t\in[0,\infty)
. More precisely we have for all
t\in[0,\infty)

\displaystyle\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x)f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x)f(t,x)\sqrt{x}\,{\rm d}x\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0});

\displaystyle\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}{\cal J}_{-1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](y,z)f^{\varepsilon}(t,y)f^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\sqrt{yz}{\rm d}y{\rm d}z=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}L[\varphi](y,z)f(t,y)f(t,z)\sqrt{yz}{\rm d}y{\rm d}z,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{3}}{\cal K}_{-1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z)f^{\varepsilon}(t,x)f^{\varepsilon}(t,y)f^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\sqrt{xyz}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y{\rm d}z=0

for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}).

Remark 1.23
.

To prove the zero limit of the cubic terms with respect to
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, our key observation lies in the symmetric structure inside the cubic terms (see the proof of Lemma
4.3
). Such a symmetric property is also observed when we consider the asymptotic expansion for the same limit (see
[
17
]
).

Case 2
: From Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL).

For Eq.(BE) (i.e. the case
\lambda=+1
), so far we could only consider measure-valued weak solutions.
In fact, global in time solutions of Eq.(BE) with the collision kernel
satisfying the above conditions (
(3)
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
) are known to exist only for measure-valued isotropic
weak solutions. (For the existence of local in time anisotropic solutions, see [10]). This is a consequence of
the dynamical Bose condensation for which one may have mass concentration in finite time (at least for some type of collision kernels).
It is also the physical counterpart of the fact that the entropy control is not enough to guarantee the

L^{1}
-weak compactness of the solution even for the case of high temperature.

Let us introduce measure spaces used for defining the measure-valued
isotropic weak solutions of Eq.(BE) and Eq.(FPL).

\bullet
Let
{\cal B}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
be the class of signed real Borel measures
F
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
satisfying

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}{\rm d}|F|(x)<\infty
where

|F|
is the total variation of
F
. For any
k\geq 0
let

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})=\Big\{F\in{\cal B}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})\,\,\big|\,\,\|F\|_{k}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1+x)^{k}{\rm d}|F|(x)<\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{k}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})=\{F\in{\cal B}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})\,\,|\,\,F\geq 0\},\quad{\cal B}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})={\cal B}^{+}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}).

Let
F\otimes F,F\otimes F\otimes F
be product measures for
F\in{\cal B}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
. To shorten notation, we denote

{\rm d}^{2}F={\rm d}(F\otimes F),{\rm d}^{3}F={\rm d}(F\otimes F\otimes F)
, i.e.

{\rm d}^{2}F(x,y)={\rm d}F(x){\rm d}F(y),{\rm d}^{3}F(x,y,z)={\rm d}F(x){\rm d}F(y){\rm d}F(z).

Definition 1.24
.

Given
\varepsilon=2\pi\hbar>0
, let

B^{\varepsilon}_{+1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
) with
\lambda=+1
.
Let
\{F_{t}^{\varepsilon}\}_{t\geq 0}\subset{\cal B}^{+}_{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
.
We say that
\{F_{t}^{\varepsilon}\}_{t\geq 0}
, or simply
F_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, is a measure-valued
isotropic weak solution
of Eq.(BE) if
F_{t}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the following
(i),(ii)
:

(i)

F_{t}^{\varepsilon}
conserves the mass and energy:

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x){\rm d}F_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x){\rm d}F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x)
for all
t\geq 0.

(ii)
For any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
,
the function
t\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi{\rm d}F_{t}^{\varepsilon}
belongs to

C^{1}([0,\infty))
and

(1.52)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi{\rm d}F_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}{\cal J}_{+1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi]{\rm d}^{2}F_{t}^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{3}}{\cal K}_{+1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi]{\rm d}^{3}F_{t}^{\varepsilon}\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,\infty).

Definition 1.25
.

Let
\{F_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}\subset{\cal B}_{1}^{+}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
.
We say that
\{F_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, or simply
F_{t}
, is a measure-valued isotropic weak solution
of Eq.(FPL) if
F_{t}
satisfies the following
(i),(ii)
:

(i)

F_{t}
conserves the mass and energy:

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x){\rm d}F_{t}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}(1,x){\rm d}F_{0}(x)

for all
t\geq 0.

(ii)
For any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
,

t\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi{\rm d}F_{t}
belongs to

C^{1}([0,\infty))
and

(1.53)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x){\rm d}F_{t}(x)=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}L[\varphi](x,y){\rm d}F_{t}(x){\rm d}F_{t}(y),\quad t\in[0,\infty)

where
L[\varphi]
is defined in
(
1.41
).

Remark 1.26
.

Our test functions

\psi({\bf v}),\varphi(x)
are chosen to be independent of time-variable
t
.
This simplified version of weak solutions is equivalent to the general version.
For instance if
f
is an
H
-solution of Eq.(FPL) in Definition
1.8
, then by Proposition
6.1
in Appendix, we have,
for any
\psi\in C^{2}_{c}([0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi(t,{\bf v})f(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f(t,{\bf v}){\partial}_{t}\psi(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\psi(t,{\bf v})Q_{L}(f)(t,{\bf v}){\rm d}{\bf v}

for all
t\in[0,\infty).

Theorem 1.27
.

[From Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL)]
Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{+1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
)
with
\lambda=+1
.
Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0,F^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in{\cal B}_{1}^{+}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
satisfy

\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}(1+x){\rm d}F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x)<\infty.

Let
F^{\varepsilon}_{t}\in{\cal B}_{1}^{+}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
be
measure-valued isotropic weak solution of Eq.(BE)
with the initial data
F_{0}^{\varepsilon}
.
Then for any sequence
{\cal E}=\{\varepsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
satisfying
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)

there exist a subsequence, still denote it as

{\cal E}
, and a
measure-valued isotropic weak solution

F_{t}
of Eq.(
FPL
) with the same initial datum
F_{0}
, such that

F^{\varepsilon}_{t}\rightharpoonup F_{t}
weakly in measure space
(as
{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0
) for all
t\geq 0
.
More precisely, for every
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x)F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x)=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}}\varphi(x){\rm d}F_{t}(x)\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in C_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0});

\displaystyle\lim_{{\cal E}\ni\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}{\cal J}_{+1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](y,z){\rm d}F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(y){\rm d}F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{2}}L[\varphi](y,z){\rm d}F_{t}(y){\rm d}F_{t}(z),

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}^{3}}{\cal K}_{+1}^{\varepsilon}[\varphi](x,y,z){\rm d}F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x){\rm d}F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(y){\rm d}F^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)=0

for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
.

Remark 1.28
.

For a
given isotropic initial datum

0\leq f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})
,
f_{0}=f_{0}(|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
,
we have two solutions of Eq.(FPL) with the same initial datum
f_{0}
. One is the weak limit of
f^{\varepsilon}
of Eq.(FD) in Theorem
1.22
, which is denote by
f(t,\cdot)
. Another one comes from the weak limit of
F^{\varepsilon}_{t}
of Eq.(BE)
in Theorem
1.27
which is a measure-valued solution
F_{t}\in{\cal B}^{+}_{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
with the initial datum
F_{0}

defined by
{\rm d}F_{0}(x)=f_{0}(x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x
. An interesting question is to ask whether
F_{t}
coincides with
f(t,\cdot)
, namely

{\rm d}F_{t}(x)=f(t,x)\sqrt{x}{\rm d}x\quad\forall\,t\in(0,\infty)\quad?

This is of course closely related to the uniqueness problem of weak solutions. We believe that the answer will be yes since there is no reason for conceiving concentrations in the FPL dynamics. But we have no idea how to prove it at the moment.

Finally let us mention the existence results for the initial value problems associated to the models Eq.(MB), Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE), which are the cornerstone for the semi-classical limit.

Theorem 1.29
.

[Existence
[
14
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
]
. Given
\varepsilon=2\pi\hbar>0
.

(1)  Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
),(
1.11
) with
\lambda=0
. Then
for any
0\leq f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}_{2}\cap L\log L({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})
, there exists a weak solution

f^{\varepsilon}
of Eq.(MB) with the initial datum
f^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
.

(2)  Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{-1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
),(
1.11
),(
1.12
)
with
\lambda=-1
. Then for any
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3})
satisfy
0\leq f_{0}^{\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
on
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}
,
there exists a weak solution
f^{\varepsilon}
of
Eq.(
FD
) with the initial datum
f^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
,
and
f^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the entropy identity (
1.16
) with
\lambda=-1
.
Besides if the initial datum
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is isotropic, i.e.

f_{0}^{\varepsilon}=f_{0}^{\varepsilon}(|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
, then the corresponding solution
f^{\varepsilon}
is also
isotropic:
f^{\varepsilon}=f^{\varepsilon}(t,|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
for all
t\geq 0
.

(3)  Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{+1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
),(
1.11
),(
1.12
)
with
\lambda=+1
. Then for any
F_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in{\cal B}_{1}^{+}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
,
there exists a measure-valued isotropic weak solution
F_{t}^{\varepsilon}
of
Eq.(BE) with the initial datum

F_{t}^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}=F_{0}^{\varepsilon}
.

Remark 1.30
.

Part (1) of the above theorem can be proved by Proposition 1.2 in
[
14
]

because (
1.23
) is almost equivalent to
the inequality (1.6) in
[
14
]
with
N=3,\gamma=-3
.

Part (2) was essentially proved in
[
21
]
. In fact,
as mentioned in Section 1, the assumption (
1.12
) implies that

\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}B^{\varepsilon}_{-1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega){\rm d}\omega
is bounded in
{\bf v-v}_{*}
.
Thus from the proof in
[
21
]
one sees that
the Eq.(FD) admits a unique mild solution
f^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),L^{1}_{2}\cap L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}))

with the initial datum
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
such that
0\leq f^{\varepsilon}\leq\varepsilon^{-3}

and
f^{\varepsilon}
conserves the mass, momentum, and energy, and satisfies
the entropy identity (
1.16
) with
\lambda=-1
. Moreover if
f_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is isotropic, then it is easily proved that the solution
f^{\varepsilon}
is also isotropic.
The only difference from the classical version is that here the collision kernel
B^{\varepsilon}_{-1}(|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|,\cos(\theta))
can not be written as the product form (e.g.)
b(\cos\theta)|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{\gamma}
.
But this is not a problem for the existence result.

Part (3) has been proven in
[
22
]
. In fact
if we write

B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)=B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(|{\bf v-v}_{*}|,\cos(\theta))

where
\theta=\arccos(|{\bf n}\cdot\omega|)
,
{\bf n}=({\bf v-v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|
, then from the assumption (
1.12
) we see that
the function
(V,\tau)\mapsto B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(V,\tau)
is continuous on

{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1]
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(V,\tau)\leq C_{\phi,\varepsilon}V\tau
for all
(V,\tau)\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1]
, where

C_{\phi,\varepsilon}=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{4}}\|\widehat{\phi}\|_{\infty}^{2}<\infty.

Thus according to
[
22
]
and the Appendix in
[
13
]
, we get the existence in the theorem.
Note also that in
[
22
]
it is also assumed that

\int_{0}^{1}B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}(V,\tau){\rm d}\tau
is strictly positive for all
V>0
, but this is only used to determine the uniqueness of equilibrium. Since in this paper we do not consider things about
equilibrium, there is no problem for the truth of part (3).

1.4.
Organization of the paper

In Section 2, we prove some preliminary estimates.
Section 3 is devoted to the proof of
the convergence from Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FPL) for general initial data.
In Section 4, we prove crucial lemmas for isotropic solutions of Eq.(FD) and Eq.(BE).
Section 5 is devoted to the proof of the convergence from Eq.(FD) to Eq.(FPL) and from Eq.(BE) to Eq.(FPL).
Some general and technical parts including those about the weak projection gradient are put in the Appendix.

2.
Preliminary lemmas for weak convergence from Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FDL).

In this section, we provide some technical lemmas which will be used to prove the convergence of weak solutions of Eq.(MB) to
H
-solutions of Eq.(FPL).

2.1.
Change of variables

To adapt the definition of weak projection gradient into the proof of the main result, in this subsection, we will focus on the change of variables in the estimates which plays the essential role in catching the entropy production.

2.1.1.
Change of spherical variable

We first introduce a change of variable for the unit vector

\omega\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
:

(2.1)

\omega=\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma,\quad\theta\in[0,\pi],\quad\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})

where
{\bf n}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
is any given vector and

{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})=\{\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\,\,|\,\,\sigma\,\bot\,{\bf n}\,\}.

Then accordingly we have, for any integrable or nonnegative Borel measurable function
\Psi
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
,
that

(2.2)

\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\Psi(\omega){\rm d}\omega=\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin\theta\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\big(\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma\big){\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}\theta\,.

Notice that we also have

(2.3)

\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma=\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi(-\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Using (
2.1
),
we may rewrite the
\omega
-representation (
1.2
) as follows:

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\bf v}^{\prime}=\sin^{2}(\theta){\bf v}+\cos^{2}(\theta){\bf v}_{*}-|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\sigma,\\
\displaystyle{\bf v}^{\prime}_{*}=\cos^{2}(\theta){\bf v}+\sin^{2}(\theta){\bf v}_{*}+|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\sigma,\\
\displaystyle\theta\in[0,\pi],\,\,\,\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n}),\,\,\,{\bf n}=({\bf v-v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|.\end{array}\right.

From this and (
2.3
) it is easily seen that
for any integrable or nonnegative Borel measurable function
\Psi
satisfying
\Psi({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\equiv\Psi({\bf v}_{*},{\bf v})

on
{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}
, we have

(2.5)

\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})\big|_{\omega=\sin(\theta){\bf n}+\cos(\theta)\sigma}{\rm d}\sigma=\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})\big|_{\omega=\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma}{\rm d}\sigma\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\pi/2].

2.1.2.
Change of variables in the integrals

We will present two key lemmas on the change of variables. To do that,
we introduce the following
variables defined for every
r>0,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and
\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
:

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\bf z}^{*}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)\equiv{\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},-\sigma);

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)={\bf z},\quad\sigma_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)=\sigma\quad{\rm for}\,\,\,|{\bf z}|<r,

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)=\big(1-2(r/|{\bf z}|)^{2}\big){\bf z}+2r\sqrt{1-(r/|{\bf z}|)^{2}}\sigma\quad{\rm for}\,\,\,|{\bf z}|\geq r,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)=2(r/|{\bf z}|)\sqrt{1-(r/|{\bf z}|)^{2}}\,{\bf n}+\big(1-2(r/|{\bf z}|)^{2}\big)\sigma\quad{\rm for}\,\,\,|{\bf z}|\geq r

with
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
.
It is easy to check that for all
r>0,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})
,

(2.10)

|{\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)|=|{\bf z}|,\quad|{\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)-{\bf z}|=2r1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}},\quad|\sigma_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)-\sigma|=\frac{2r}{|{\bf z}|}1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}}.

One may see that (
2.6
)-(
2.9
) exactly come from the change of variables in the associated integrals:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\Psi({\bf w},{\bf w}_{*},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\geq 0
be a Borel measurable functions on
{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}
. Suppose that
\Psi
is symmetric with respective to the first two variables:

\Psi({\bf w},{\bf w}_{*},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\Psi({\bf w}_{*},{\bf w},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}).

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}B^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\big|_{\omega-{\rm rep.}}{\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq\varepsilon r\}}}{|{\bf z}|(2\varepsilon r)^{2}}\varrho(r)\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}r{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf w}

where
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|,

{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}={\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}({\bf z},\sigma)
is defined in
(
2.7
)-(
2.8
), and, according to (
1.11
), the function

(2.12)

\varrho(r):=2\pi r^{3}|\widehat{\phi}(r)|^{2}\qquad\mbox{satisfies}\,\,\quad\int_{0}^{\infty}\varrho(r){\rm d}r=1.

Furthermore, if inaddition that
\Psi({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\equiv 0
, then

(2.13)

\displaystyle\int_{{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}B^{\varepsilon}_{0}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\big|_{\omega-{\rm rep.}}{\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|(2\varepsilon r)^{2}}\varrho(r)\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}r{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf w}.

Proof.

Thanks to (
1.7
), (
1.25
), and the assumption on
\Psi
, and
using change of variables

({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\frac{1}{2}({\bf w}+{\bf z},{\bf w}-{\bf z})
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm the\,\,l.h.s.\,\,of\,\,(\ref{2.14})}=\int_{{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf v}-{\bf v}^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\Psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})\big|_{\sigma-{\rm rep.}}{\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf v}{\rm d}{\bf v}_{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\int_{{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}\bigg(\underbrace{\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}(1-{\bf n}\cdot\sigma)}\,\Big)^{2}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+|{\bf z}|\sigma}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-|{\bf z}|\sigma}{2},\frac{{\bf w}+{\bf z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma}_{\mathcal{I}}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf w}{\rm d}{\bf z},

where
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
.
For the inner integral
\mathcal{I}
, we have with (
2.2
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin(\theta)\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon}\sin(\theta/2)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+|{\bf z}|(\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma)}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-|{\bf z}|(\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma)}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\theta

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{1}s\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon^{4}}\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon}s\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+(1-2s^{2}){\bf z}+2|{\bf z}|\sqrt{1-s^{2}}s\sigma}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-((1-2s^{2}){\bf z}+2|{\bf z}|\sqrt{1-s^{2}}s\sigma)}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\frac{|{\bf z}|}{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{(\varepsilon r)^{2}}r^{3}\widehat{\phi}(r)^{2}\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq\varepsilon r\}}}{(2\varepsilon r)^{2}}\varrho(r)\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(\frac{{\bf w}+{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w}-{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}}{2},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}r.

Thus (
2.11
) holds true. The equality (
2.13
) is due to the
fact that if
|{\bf z}|<\varepsilon r
then
{\bf z}_{\varepsilon r}={\bf z}
so that the integrand is zero.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\Psi({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf u}),\psi(r,{\bf z})
be Borel measurable functions on
{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}

and
(0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}

respectively, and they are either nonnegative or such that the following integrals are absolutely convergent for all
r>0
. Then for almost every

r>0
,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf z}_{r}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z}^{*}_{r},\sigma_{r},{\bf z}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi(|{\bf z}|,{\bf z}_{r}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}=2\pi\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi(|{\bf z}|,{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z},

where
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
and
{\bf z}_{r}={\bf z}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma),{\bf z}^{*}_{r}={\bf z}^{*}_{r}({\bf z},\sigma),\sigma_{r}=\sigma_{r}({\bf z},\sigma)
are defined in (
2.6
)-(
2.9
).

Proof.

The second equality (
2.15
) follows from (
2.14
)
and the identity
|{\bf z}^{*}_{r}|=|{\bf z}|.
So we need only to prove the first equality (
2.14
).

Without loss of generality we may assume that
\Psi
is nonnegative.
By monotone approximation
\Psi_{n}({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf u})=(\Psi({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf u})\wedge n)e^{-(|{\bf z}|+|{\bf u}|)/n}
, we may also assume that
the integrals in (
2.14
) are bounded in
r\in(0,\infty)
.
For any Borel measurable function
\varphi\in L^{1}([0,\infty))
, we have by
spherical coordinate transform
(with
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
) that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\varphi\Big(|{\bf z}|\sqrt{\frac{1-{\bf n}\cdot\omega}{2}}\Big)\Psi\Big({\bf z},\frac{\omega-({\bf n}\cdot\omega){\bf n}}{\sqrt{1-({\bf n}\cdot\omega)^{2}}},|{\bf z}|\omega\Big){\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\varphi\Big(|{\bf z}|\sqrt{\frac{1-\omega\cdot{\bf n}}{2}}\Big)\Psi\Big(|{\bf z}|\omega,\frac{{\bf n}-(\omega\cdot{\bf n})\omega}{\sqrt{1-(\omega\cdot{\bf n})^{2}}},{\bf z}\Big){\rm d}\omega{\rm d}{\bf z}.

Then using (
2.2
) and change of variables
t=\frac{r}{|{\bf z}|}
and
\sigma\to-\sigma
, we
compute

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin(\theta)\varphi(|{\bf z}|\sin(\theta/2))\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(|{\bf z}|\big(\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma\big),{\bf n}\sin(\theta)-\cos(\theta)\sigma,{\bf z}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\theta{\rm d}{\bf z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{0}^{1}t\varphi(|{\bf z}|t)\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big(|{\bf z}|\big((1-2t^{2}){\bf n}+2t\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\sigma\big),{\bf n}2t\sqrt{1-t^{2}}-(1-2t^{2})\sigma,{\bf z}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}t{\rm d}{\bf z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(r)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z}^{*}_{r},\sigma_{r},{\bf z}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}\bigg){\rm d}r.

On the other hand using (
2.2
) we also have

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin(\theta)\varphi(|{\bf z}|\sin(\theta/2))\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\big({\bf z},\sigma,|{\bf z}|(\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma)\big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\theta{\rm d}{\bf z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|{\bf z}|\int_{0}^{1}t\varphi(|{\bf z}|t)\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi\Big({\bf z},\sigma,|{\bf z}|\big((1-2t^{2}){\bf n}+2t\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\sigma\big)\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}t{\rm d}{\bf z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(r)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf z}_{r}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}\bigg){\rm d}r.

Since
\varphi\in L^{1}([0,\infty))
is arbitrary, this implies that

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z},\sigma,{\bf z}_{r}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}1_{\{|{\bf z}|\geq r\}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Psi({\bf z}^{*}_{r},\sigma_{r},{\bf z}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}

for almost every
r>0
. By definition of

{\bf z}_{r},{\bf z}^{*}_{r},\sigma_{r}
we have
{\bf z}_{r}={\bf z}^{*}_{r}={\bf z},\sigma_{r}=\sigma
for all
|{\bf z}|<r
, so this
gives the equality (
2.14
).
∎

2.2.
Convergence of
L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]

We begin with two lemmas.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\psi\in C^{2}_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,

\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\psi({\bf v}^{\prime})+\psi({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})-\psi({\bf v})-\psi({\bf v}_{*}),

\displaystyle\|D^{2}\psi\|_{*}:=\sup_{{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2})^{1/4}}\sup_{|{\bf u}|\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}}\big|D^{2}\psi({\bf u})-\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}(D^{2}\psi({\bf 0})){\rm I}\big|,

\displaystyle C_{\psi}^{*}({\bf v,v}_{*}):=\min\big\{\|D^{2}\psi\|_{\infty},\,\|D^{2}\psi\|_{*}(1+|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2})^{1/4}\big\}

where
{\rm I}=(\delta_{ij})_{3\times 3}
is the
unit matrix.
Then for all
{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
we have

|\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})|\leq C_{\psi}^{*}({\bf v,v}_{*})|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}|\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)|\quad\forall\,\omega\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},

where
\theta=\arccos({\bf n}\cdot\omega)\in[0,\pi]
,
{\bf n}=({\bf v-v}_{*})/|{\bf v-v}_{*}|
. Moreover,
with (
2.4
) we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\sigma\bigg|\leq 8\pi C_{\psi}^{*}({\bf v,v}_{*})|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta)\cos^{2}(\theta)\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\pi].

Proof.

Let

\psi_{h}({\bf v})=\psi({\bf v})-h|{\bf v}|^{2}/2
with
h\in{{\mathbb{R}}}.

Then

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{h}({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}),\quad\nabla\psi_{h}({\bf v})=\nabla\psi({\bf v})-h{\bf v},

(2.16)

\displaystyle D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf v})=D^{2}\psi({\bf v})-h{\rm I}.

By writing

\Delta\psi_{h}=({\psi_{h}}^{\prime}-\psi_{h})-({\psi_{h}}_{*}-{\psi_{h}}_{*}^{\prime})
and using
{\bf v}_{*}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}={\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}
, we derive that

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{h}({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!\int_{0}^{1}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime})^{\tau}D^{2}\psi_{h}(\xi_{t,s})({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}){\rm d}s{\rm d}t

where
\xi_{t,s}={\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}+t({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v})+s({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}),|\xi_{t,s}|\leq\max\{|{\bf v}|,|{\bf v}^{\prime}|,|{\bf v}_{*}|,|{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}|\}\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}\,.

Since

|{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}||{\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}|=|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}|\sin(\theta)|\cos(\theta)|
, this gives

\displaystyle|\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})|\leq\Big(\sup_{|{\bf u}|\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}}|D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf u})|\Big)|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}|\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)|.

From this and (
2.16
) one sees that the first inequality in the lemma follows from
taking
h=0
and using the following inequality

(2.17)

\sup_{|{\bf u}|\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}}|D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf u})|\leq\|D^{2}\psi\|_{*}(1+|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2})^{1/4}\quad{\rm with}\quad h=\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}(D^{2}\psi({\bf 0})).

To prove the second inequality in the lemma, we set
\omega=\cos(\theta){\bf n}+\sin(\theta)\sigma
with
\theta\in[0,\pi]
and
\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})
.
By writing

\Delta\psi_{h}=(\psi_{h}^{\prime}-\psi_{h})+({\psi_{h}}_{*}^{\prime}-{\psi_{h}}_{*})
and using

{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*}=-({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v})
, we derive that

\displaystyle\Delta\psi_{h}({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\nabla\psi_{h}({\bf v})-\nabla\psi_{h}({\bf v}_{*})\big)\cdot({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v})^{\tau}D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf v}+t({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}))({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}){\rm d}t

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*})^{\tau}D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf v}_{*}+t({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*}))({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}t\,.

Using (
2.4
) we have
{\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}=-({\bf v-v}_{*})\cos^{2}(\theta)-|{\bf v-v}_{*}|\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\sigma
. Since

\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}{\bf a}\cdot\sigma{\rm d}\sigma=0
, it follows that

(2.18)

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\sigma=-2\pi\big(\nabla\psi_{h}({\bf v})-\nabla\psi_{h}({\bf v}_{*})\big)\cdot({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*})\cos^{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle+\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v})^{\tau}D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf v}+t({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}))({\bf v}^{\prime}-{\bf v}){\rm d}t{\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle+\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*})^{\tau}D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf v}_{*}+t({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*}))({\bf v}_{*}^{\prime}-{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}t{\rm d}\sigma

and thus

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\sigma\bigg|\ \leq 4\pi\Big(\sup_{|{\bf u}|\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}}|D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf u})|\Big)|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}\cos^{2}(\theta).

Similar argument applied to the decomposition

\Delta\psi_{h}=(\psi_{h}^{\prime}-{\psi_{h}}_{*})+({\psi_{h}}_{*}^{\prime}-\psi_{h})
also gives

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Delta\psi({\bf v}^{\prime},{\bf v}_{*}^{\prime},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}){\rm d}\sigma\Big|\ \leq 4\pi\Big(\sup_{|{\bf u}|\leq\sqrt{|{\bf v}|^{2}+|{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}}}|D^{2}\psi_{h}({\bf u})|\Big)|{\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*}|^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta).

Since
\min\{\cos^{2}(\theta),\sin^{2}(\theta)\}\leq 2\cos^{2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\theta)
, this together with (
2.17
) gives the second inequality.
∎

It should be noted that for an isotropic function

\psi({\bf v})=\varphi(|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)
with
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
, we have

D^{2}\psi({\bf v})-\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}(D^{2}\psi({\bf 0})){\rm I}=\varphi^{\prime\prime}(|{\bf v}|^{2}/2){\bf v}\otimes{\bf v}+[\varphi^{\prime}(|{\bf v}|^{2}/2)-\varphi^{\prime}(0)]{\rm I}

Using
the inequality
|\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)-\varphi^{\prime}(0)|\leq\int_{0}^{\rho}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(r)|{\rm d}r\leq\big(\sup\limits_{0\leq r\leq\rho}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(r)|r^{3/4}\big)4\rho^{1/4}
gives

\displaystyle\big|D^{2}\psi({\bf u})-\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}(D^{2}\psi({\bf 0})){\rm I}\big|\leq 8\Big(\sup_{r\geq 0}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(r)|r^{3/4}\Big)|{\bf u}|^{1/2}

hence, by definition of
\|D^{2}\psi\|_{*}
,

(2.19)

\|D^{2}\psi\|_{*}\leq 8\sup_{r\geq 0}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(r)|r^{3/4}.

This inequality will be used to prove the conservation of energy.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\widehat{\phi}
satisfy (
1.11
) and let

A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi}}(\varepsilon)=\sup_{\rho\geq 0}(\rho^{1/2}\wedge 1)\int_{\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}}^{\infty}r^{3}|\widehat{\phi}(r)|^{2}{\rm d}r,\quad A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi},\alpha}(\varepsilon)=\sup_{\rho>0}(\rho^{1/2}\wedge 1)\int_{0}^{\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}r\Big)^{\alpha}r^{3}|\widehat{\phi}(r)|^{2}{\rm d}r

where
\alpha>0
is a constant. Then

0\leq A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi}}(\varepsilon),A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi},\alpha}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}
for all
\varepsilon>0,

(2.20)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi}}(\varepsilon)=0,\qquad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}A^{*}_{\widehat{\phi},\alpha}(\varepsilon)=0,

and for all
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
,

(2.21)

0\leq R_{\varepsilon}(|{\bf z}|):=\frac{1}{2\pi|{\bf z}|^{3}}-\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{|{\bf z}|\cos^{3}(\theta)\sin(\theta)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\Big|\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf z}|\cos(\theta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big|^{2}{\rm d}\theta\leq\frac{A_{\widehat{\phi}}^{*}(\varepsilon)}{|{\bf z}|^{3}(|{\bf z}|^{1/2}\wedge 1)},

(2.22)

\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{|{\bf z}|\cos^{3+\alpha}(\theta)\sin(\theta)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\Big|\widehat{\phi}\Big(\frac{|{\bf z}|\cos(\theta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big|^{2}{\rm d}\theta\leq\frac{A_{\widehat{\phi},\alpha}^{*}(\varepsilon)}{|{\bf z}|^{3}(|{\bf z}|^{1/2}\wedge 1)}.

Proof.

The convergence (
2.20
) is easily proved by using the integrability

\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{3}|\widehat{\phi}(r)|^{2}{\rm d}r=\frac{1}{2\pi}
and dominated convergence theorem.
The inequalities (
2.21
) and (
2.22
) follow from change of variables and the definition of

A_{\widehat{\phi}}^{*}(\varepsilon),A_{\widehat{\phi},\alpha}^{*}(\varepsilon)
.
∎

Now we are in a position to prove the convergence of
L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let

B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
be given by (
(3)
), (
1.11
) with
\lambda\in\{0,-1,+1\}
, and let
L[\psi]({\bf v,v}_{*}),L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})
be defined in (
1.30
), (
1.36
) for
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.
Then for all
{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
with
{\bf v}\neq{\bf v}_{*}
,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}L^{\varepsilon}_{0}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=L[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}),

(2.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\{|L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})|,\,|L[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})|\}\leq 16\|D^{2}\psi\|_{\infty}\frac{1}{|{\bf v-v}_{*}|}.

Proof.

By definition of
B^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf v}-{\bf v}_{*},\omega)
and (
1.36
) we have

(2.25)

L^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=L^{\varepsilon}_{0}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})+\lambda E^{\varepsilon}[\psi]({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})

where
