\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}\phi(|{\bf x}|)\widehat{\varphi}({\bf x}){\rm d}{\bf x}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\rho^{2}\phi(\rho)\widehat{\varphi}(\rho\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\rho=\lim\limits_{R\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\rho^{2}\phi_{R}(\rho)\widehat{\varphi}(\rho\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\rho.

On the other hand for any
0<R<\infty
we compute

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi|\leq R}\widehat{\phi}(|\xi|)\varphi(\xi){\rm d}\xi=\int_{0}^{R}r^{2}\widehat{\phi}(r)\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}(2\pi)^{-3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\widehat{\varphi}({\bf x})e^{{\rm i}{\bf x}\cdot r\omega}{\rm d}{\bf x}{\rm d}\omega{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R}r^{2}\widehat{\phi}(r)(2\pi)^{-3}\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\widehat{\varphi}({\bf x})\frac{4\pi}{r|{\bf x}|}\sin(r|{\bf x}|){\rm d}{\bf x}{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\rho^{2}\phi_{R}(\rho)\widehat{\varphi}(\rho\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}\rho.

Since
\widehat{\phi}(|\cdot|)\varphi\in L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,
letting
R\to\infty
we deduce from this and (
6.7
) that

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}\widehat{\phi}(|\xi|)\varphi(\xi){\rm d}\xi=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}}\phi(|{\bf x}|)\widehat{\varphi}({\bf x}){\rm d}{\bf x}\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in{\cal S}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}).

According to the Fourier transform of generalized functions,
\widehat{\phi}(|\xi|)
is the Fourier transform of
\phi(|{\bf x}|)
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Now we turn to the second part of the proposition. Assume that
r_{0}=0
and
\widehat{\phi}(r)\geq 0

for all
r\in(0,\infty)
. Then
r\mapsto r\widehat{\phi}(r)
is monotone in
(0,\infty)
. Since

\lim\limits_{r\to\infty}r\widehat{\phi}(r)=0
and
\widehat{\phi}(r)\not\equiv 0
, this implies that

r\mapsto r\widehat{\phi}(r)
is monotone non-increasing in
(0,\infty)
. Therefore
using the second mean-value formula of integrals we have
for any
R>\delta>0
that

(6.8)

\int_{\delta}^{R}r\widehat{\phi}(r)\sin(\rho r){\rm d}r\geq\frac{\delta\widehat{\phi}(\delta)}{\rho}(\cos(\rho\delta)-1)\geq-\,\frac{\rho}{2}\delta^{3}\widehat{\phi}(\delta).

Since the assumption (
1.11
) implies that
r\mapsto r^{3/2}\widehat{\phi}(r)
belongs to
L^{1}((0,1))
, it follows that there is a sequence
0<\delta_{n}\to 0\,(n\to\infty)
such that

\delta_{n}^{3}\widehat{\phi}(\delta_{n})\to 0\,(n\to\infty).
From this and (
6.8
) we obtain

\int_{0}^{R}r\widehat{\phi}(r)\sin(\rho r){\rm d}r=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\delta_{n}}^{R}r\widehat{\phi}(r)\sin(\rho r){\rm d}r\geq 0\qquad\forall\,\rho>0,\,R>0.

Thus we conclude
\phi(\rho)=\lim\limits_{R\to\infty}\phi_{R}(\rho)\geq 0
for all
\rho\in(0,\infty).

∎

6.3.
Weak projection gradient: definition and properties

Let us first list some basic notations which are widely used throughout this section.

\bullet
We recall that
\Pi({\bf z})=\Pi({\bf n}):={\rm I}-{\bf n}\otimes{\bf n}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3\times 3}
where
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
.

\bullet
The vertical plane
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})
is defined by

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})=\{{\bf h}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\,\,|\,\,{\bf z}\cdot{\bf h}=0\},\quad{\bf z}\neq{\bf 0}.

\bullet

Y
is a Borel set in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{N}
. In our research problems shown above,
Y

has been taken
[0,\infty)\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
and
[0,\infty)
.

Test function spaces for
scalar and vector-valued cases are defined by

(6.9)

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})=\big\{\psi\in C_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\,\,\big|\,\,{\rm for\,\,any\,\,}\,{\bf y}\in Y,\,\,\psi({\bf y},{\bf 0})=0,\,\,{\bf z}\mapsto\psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}),

\displaystyle{\rm and}\,\,\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z})|<\infty\big\},

(6.10)

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})=\big\{\Psi=(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3})^{\tau}\in C_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})\,\,\big|\,\,{\rm for\,\,any\,\,}\,{\bf y}\in Y,\,\,\Psi({\bf y},{\bf 0})={\bf 0},

\displaystyle{\bf z}\mapsto\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}),\,{\rm and}\,\,\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|\Psi^{\prime}_{{\bf z}}({\bf y},{\bf z})|<\infty\big\}

where
\Psi^{\prime}_{{\bf z}}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\big(\frac{\partial\Psi_{i}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{\partial z_{j}}\big)_{3\times 3}
,

|\Psi^{\prime}_{{\bf z}}({\bf y},{\bf z})|=\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}|\frac{\partial\Psi_{i}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{\partial z_{j}}|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

Remark 6.3
.

(1)
Denote by
\nabla_{\bf z}=(\partial_{z_{1}},\partial_{z_{2}},\partial_{z_{3}})^{\tau}
the
gradient operator of functions on
{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
. Using the projection
\Pi({\bf z})
we have an orthogonal decomposition

\nabla_{\bf z}=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}+({\bf n}\cdot\nabla_{\bf z}){\bf n}
, i.e.

(6.11)

\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})+({\bf n}\cdot\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})){\bf n},\quad\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})\perp{\bf n}

for all
F\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
. This gives the differentiation of
F
at
{\bf z}
along the vertical plane
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})
:

(6.12)

F({\bf z}+{\bf h})-F({\bf z})=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})\cdot{\bf h}+o({\bf h}),\quad{\bf h}\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})

In view of (
6.12
), we call
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z})
the gradient of
F
at

{\bf z}
along the vertical plan
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})
, or simply, the
projection gradient
of
F
at
{\bf z}
.
Let
\Psi\in C^{1}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
. Using the identity

(6.13)

{\bf a}\cdot\Pi({\bf z}){\bf b}=\Pi({\bf z}){\bf a}\cdot{\bf b}={\bf b}\cdot\Pi({\bf z}){\bf a},\quad{\bf a},{\bf b}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}

and
integration by parts we have

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf z})\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}=-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}.

(2) For all
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, we have

(6.14)

\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})=\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})-{\bf n}^{\tau}\Psi^{\prime}_{{\bf z}}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\bf n}-2\frac{\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n},

where

{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|,\,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
.
Since
\Psi({\bf y},{\bf 0})={\bf 0}
, the quotient
\frac{\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}
makes sense and is bounded:

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}}\frac{|\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})|}{|{\bf z}|}\leq\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|\Psi_{{\bf z}}^{\prime}({\bf y},{\bf z})|<\infty.

Thus
\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})
is bounded and has compact support
in
Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3}
.

6.3.1.
Definition of the weak projection gradients

Definition 6.4
.

Let
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
. We say that
F({\bf y,z})

has a weak projection gradient
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})

in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
, if
{\bf D}\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
and

(6.15)

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

for all
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

In this case, we denote

{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}).

Several remarks are in order.

Remark 6.5
.

(1) The perpendicular property
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})
is necessary for
{\bf D}\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
satisfying (
6.15
).
In fact, by inserting

\Psi_{\varepsilon}({\bf y,z}):=\big({\rm I}-\frac{{\bf z}{\bf z}^{\tau}}{\varepsilon^{2}+|{\bf z}|^{2}}\big)\Psi({\bf y,z})
with
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
into (
6.15
) and letting
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
we conclude
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot{\bf z}=0
for a.e.
({\bf y},{\bf z})\in Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
.

(2) It is obvious that, up to a set of measure zero,
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
is
uniquely determined by the identity (
6.15
).

(3) From (
6.14
) one sees that the condition
\Psi({\bf y},{\bf 0})={\bf 0}
for the test functions is only used to bound
\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})
; it can be removed if
F
satisfies that
F({\bf y},{\bf z})/|{\bf z}|\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.

To adapt Definition
6.4
to our problem, we observe that for
a smooth function
F({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})
, let

\bar{F}({\bf w,z})=F\big(\frac{\bf w+z}{2},\frac{\bf w-z}{2}\big),
then
it holds the following relation:

\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\big(\nabla_{{\bf v}}-\nabla_{{\bf v}_{*}}\big)F({\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})=2\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\bar{F}({\bf w,z})\big|_{{\bf w}={\bf v+v}_{*},\,{\bf z}={\bf v-v}_{*}}.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}})
. We say that
F({\bf y},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})

has the weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}F({\bf y},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*})
in
{\bf v-v}_{*}\neq{\bf 0}
, if
the function

\bar{F}({\bf y},{\bf w},{\bf z}):=F\big({\bf y},\frac{{\bf w+z}}{2},\frac{{\bf w-z}}{2}\big)

has the weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\bar{F}({\bf y},{\bf w},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
. In this case we define

(6.16)

\Pi({\bf v-v}_{*})\nabla_{{\bf v-v}_{*}}F({\bf y},{\bf v},{\bf v}_{*}):=2\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\bar{F}({\bf y},{\bf w},{\bf z})\big|_{{\bf w}={\bf v+v}_{*},\,{\bf z}={\bf v-v}_{*}}.

From the relation between the two types of weak projection gradients one sees that
in order to study the weak projection gradients
defined in the second definition, it needs only to study the weak projection gradients
defined in the first definition.

Let us first show that the vector-valued test function space
in Definition
6.4
can be reduced to the scalar test function space.

Lemma 6.7
.

A function
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
has the weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
, if and only if there exists a vector-valued function

{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})

such that
{\bf D}\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
and the equality

(6.17)

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z})-2\frac{\psi({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

holds for all
\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

Moreover, if (
6.17
) holds for all
\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, then
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
a.e. on
Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
.

The proof follows easily from Definition
6.4

and the implication relation that if
\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})

then
\psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\bf a}
belongs to

{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
for all constant vector
{\bf a}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
. We omit the details here.

By definition of the projection
\Pi({\bf z})
we have

\Pi({\bf z}){\bf z}={\bf 0}
for all
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
. From this
it is easily deduced the following equalities that are often used:
For any
a\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
and any
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
,

(6.18)

\displaystyle\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\big(a(|{\bf z}|)\psi({\bf z})\big)=a(|{\bf z}|)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf z})\qquad\forall\,\psi\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})

(6.19)

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})a(|{\bf z}|)\psi({\bf z})=a(|{\bf z}|)\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\psi({\bf z})\qquad\forall\,\psi\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

6.3.2.
Some properties of weak projection gradients

The first part of the following lemma shows that
if in addition that
F({\bf y},{\bf z})/|{\bf z}|\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, then the
condition
\psi({\bf y},{\bf 0})=0
in test function space
{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
can be removed.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})

has the weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
.

(a) If
F({\bf y},{\bf z})/|{\bf z}|\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, then for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, there is a
null set
Z_{\psi}\subset Y
such that
for all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\psi}

(6.20)

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\psi({\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}=-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z})\nabla\psi({\bf z})-2\frac{\psi({\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}{\bf z}.

(b) Let
a\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
. If
a(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})
and

a(|{\bf z}|)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
both
belong to
L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, then
a(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})

also has the
weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\big(a(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big)
in

{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and it holds

(6.21)

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\big(a(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big)=a(|{\bf z}|)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}).

Proof.

(a): First of all from the assumptions we have
for any
R_{1},R_{2}\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
that

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|<R_{1}}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf y}=\int_{|{\bf y}|<R_{1},|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}<\infty,

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|<R_{1}}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}|F({\bf y},{\bf z})|\big(1+\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|}\big){\rm d}{\bf z}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf y}=\int_{|{\bf y}|<R_{1},|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}|F({\bf y},{\bf z})|\big(1+\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|}\big){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}<\infty.

Therefore all integrals in the rest of the proof are absolutely convergent and

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}<\infty,\quad\int_{|{\bf z}|<R_{2}}|F({\bf y},{\bf z})|\big(1+\frac{1}{|{\bf z}|}\big){\rm d}{\bf z}<\infty

for all
R_{2}\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
and all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{0}
, where

Z_{0}
is a common null set in
Y
.

Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0})
satisfy

0\leq\zeta(\cdot)\leq 1
on
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
, and
\zeta(r)=0
for all
r\in[0,1]
,

\zeta(r)=1
for all
r\geq 2
. Let

\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)=\zeta(|{\bf z}|/\delta),\delta>0
.
Take any
\varphi\in C_{c}(Y)
and
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.
Let

\psi_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\varphi({\bf y})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)\psi({\bf z}).

Then
\psi_{\delta}\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
and thanks to the identity (
6.18
) we have

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\psi_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\varphi({\bf y})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla\psi({\bf z})
and so, by
Lemma
6.7
,

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\varphi({\bf y})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)\psi({\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\varphi({\bf y})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\psi({\bf z})-2\frac{\psi({\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

By definition of the test functions and the fact
that
\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)=1
for all

{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
, dominated convergence theorem yields that

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\varphi({\bf y})\psi({\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\varphi({\bf y})\Big(\Pi({\bf z})\nabla\psi({\bf z})-2\frac{\psi({\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Since
\varphi\in C_{c}(Y)
is arbitrary, there is a null set
Z_{\psi}\subset Y

such that (
6.20
) holds for all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\psi}
.

(b): For any
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, the function

\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)a(|{\bf z}|)\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})

(
\delta>0
) belongs to
{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
and using (
6.18
) we have

\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)a(|{\bf z}|)\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})=\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)a(|{\bf z}|)\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})

for all
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and so

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)a(|{\bf z}|)\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)a(|{\bf z}|)\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

By the assumptions in (b) and recalling that
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
,
letting
\delta\to 0^{+}
we obtain

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})a(|{\bf z}|)\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})a(|{\bf z}|)\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

This proves (
6.21
) by definition of weak projection gradients.
∎

Note that for the case
a(r)=r^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
, the condition “

|{\bf z}|^{\alpha}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
and

|{\bf z}|^{\alpha}\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
both
belong to
L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
” is satisfied automatically and thus for this case the
conclusion of the lemma holds true.
Whereas for the case
a(r)=r^{-\alpha}
(
\alpha>0
), the condition “

|{\bf z}|^{-\alpha}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
and

|{\bf z}|^{-\alpha}\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
both
belong to
L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
” should be checked out in practice.

The following two lemmas are important:

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose that
F\in L^{2}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
has the weak
projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in

{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and that

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
belongs to
L^{2}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.
Then
F^{2}
also has the weak
projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F^{2}({\bf y},{\bf z})
in

{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}

and

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F^{2}({\bf y},{\bf z})=2F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}).

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
0<F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
has the weak
projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and satisfies

\inf_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R_{1},|{\bf z}|\leq R_{2}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})>0\qquad\forall\,0<R_{1},R_{2}<\infty,

(6.22)

\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\,\,{\rm belongs\,\,to}\,\,\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

Then
\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}
has also the weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and it holds

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}=\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}.

To prove the two lemmas, we first make a common preparation. Note that the assumption

F\in L^{2}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
implies that
F
satisfies (
6.22
).

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
j(t)=c\exp(\frac{-1}{1-t})1_{\{t<1\}}
and

J({\bf u})=j(|{\bf u}|^{2})
with

c>0
such that

\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}J({\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}=1.

Set

J_{\delta}({\bf u})=\delta^{-3}J(\delta^{-1}{\bf u}),\delta>0.

Suppose
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
satisfies (
6.22
) and has the weak
projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
. Let

F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=(J_{\delta}*F({\bf y},\cdot))({\bf z}).

Then for any any
\delta>0
and any
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
, there
is a null set
Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}\subset Y
, such that for all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}
,

(6.23)

\displaystyle\qquad\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})

\displaystyle=\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\big(F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big)\Big\{\big(\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\frac{-\Pi({\bf z}){\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-2J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big\}{\rm d}{\bf u},

and

(6.24)

\displaystyle\qquad\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})

\displaystyle=\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\Big(\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\Big)\Big\{\big(\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\big(-\Pi({\bf z}){\bf u}\big)-2J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\Big\}{\rm d}{\bf u}.

Proof.

Since
({\bf y},{\bf u})\mapsto\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf u})}{|{\bf u}|}

belongs to
L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, applying Lemma
6.8
(a) to
({\bf y},{\bf u})\mapsto F({\bf y},{\bf u})
and taking

{\bf u}\mapsto J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u})
as a test function
with
{\bf z},\delta
fixed,
we have, for a null set
Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}\subset Y
that

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u})\Pi({\bf u})\nabla_{\bf u}F({\bf y},{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}=-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf u})\Big(\Pi({\bf u})\nabla_{{\bf u}}(J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u}))-2\frac{J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u})}{|{\bf u}|^{2}}{\bf u}\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf u})\nabla_{{\bf z}}J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}-\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf u})\Big(\big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf u}}{|{\bf u}|}\big)\frac{{\bf u}}{|{\bf u}|}-2J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u})\frac{{\bf u}}{|{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}

for all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}
.
This gives

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf u})\nabla_{{\bf z}}J_{\delta}({\bf z}-{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle=\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\Big(\big(\delta^{-1}\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-2J({\bf u})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}.

and so

\displaystyle\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})

\displaystyle=\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\Big(\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Big){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\Big\{\big(\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\delta^{-1}\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-2J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big\}{\rm d}{\bf u}

for all
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}
. Here we have used
\Pi({\bf z})^{2}=\Pi({\bf z})
.

To prove (
6.23
) and (
6.24
), we then need only to prove the following claim: for any Borel measurable function

G(r,s,t)
on
[0,1]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}\times[0,\infty)
satisfying

|G(r,s,t)|\leq C\frac{1}{t^{\alpha}}
for all
(r,s,t)\in[0,1]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}\times(0,\infty)

for some constants
0<C<\infty,\alpha<3
, we have

(6.25)

\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}G\big(|{\bf u}|,{\bf u}\cdot({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}),|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|\big)\Pi({\bf z})({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}={\bf 0},\quad{\bf z}\neq{\bf 0},\delta>0.

In fact, from the assumption on
G
we see that the integral below is absolutely
convergent, and recalling
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
,
\Pi({\bf z}){\bf z}={\bf 0}
, and (
2.2
) we
compute

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}G\big(|{\bf u}|,{\bf u}\cdot({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}),|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|\big)\Pi({\bf z})({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}=-\delta\int_{0}^{1}r^{3}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle\times G\big(r,r|{\bf z}|\cos(\theta)-\delta r^{2},\sqrt{|{\bf z}|^{2}-2\delta|{\bf z}|r\cos(\theta)+\delta^{2}r^{2}}\big)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\sigma{\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}\theta{\rm d}r={\bf 0},

where in the last equality we have used
\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\sigma{\rm d}\sigma={\bf 0}.

Now since

J({\bf u})=j(|{\bf u}|^{2}),\nabla J({\bf u})=2j^{\prime}(|{\bf u}|^{2}){\bf u}
, applying (
6.25
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}\Big\{\big(\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\delta^{-1}\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-2J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big\}{\rm d}{\bf u}={\bf 0},

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\Big\{\big(\nabla J({\bf u})\cdot\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\big)\delta^{-1}\Pi({\bf z})({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-2J({\bf u})\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\Big\}{\rm d}{\bf u}={\bf 0}.

These together with
\delta^{-1}\Pi({\bf z})({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})=\Pi({\bf z})(-{\bf u})
yield (
6.23
) and (
6.24
). ∎

We also need the following elementary property:

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty,l\in{{\mathbb{N}}},g\in L^{p}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}}^{l})
. Then
for all
0<R<\infty
we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|g({\bf y},{\bf z}+{\bf h})-g({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{p}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0\quad{\rm as}\quad{\bf h}\to{\bf 0},

\displaystyle\sup_{|{\bf h}|<1}\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|g({\bf y},{\bf z}+{\bf h})-g({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{p}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\leq 2^{p}\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R+1}|g({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{p}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Let
F
satisfy the assumptions in Lemma
6.9
and let
F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=(J_{\delta}*F({\bf y},\cdot))({\bf z})
. Using Lemma
6.12
, one has

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\delta<1}\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\leq\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R+1}|F({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y},

\displaystyle\int_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-F({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0\quad{\rm as}\quad\delta\to 0^{+}.

Since
F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})
is smooth in
{\bf z}
, we have

\nabla_{{\bf z}}(F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})^{2})=2F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})

and so for any
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})

(6.26)

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot 2F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F^{2}_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Convergence of l.h.s of (
6.26
):  By change of variables and simple estimates
it is not difficult to prove that there is a constant
0<C<\infty
such that

\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}\Big|\frac{\Pi({\bf z}){\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}\Big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf u}\leq\frac{C}{|{\bf z}|^{2}},\quad\int_{|{\bf u}|<1}\Big|\Pi({\bf z})\frac{{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|^{2}}\Big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf u}\leq\frac{C}{|{\bf z}|^{2}},\quad\forall\,\delta>0,\,{\bf z}\neq{\bf 0}.

Then using (
6.23
) and

|\Pi({\bf z}){\bf h}|\leq|{\bf h}|
,
we obtain that, for all
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},0<\delta<1
and all

{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}
,

\displaystyle\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\big|\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{|{\bf z}|^{2}}\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\big|F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf u}.

From this and definition of
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
which implies that

|\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})|\leq C_{\Psi}|{\bf z}|
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}|\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})|^{2}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|<R,|{\bf z}|<R}\big|\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+C_{\Psi}\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf u}\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}),

where we have used Lemma
6.12
.
This implies the desired convergence:

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi\cdot 2F_{\delta}\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi\cdot 2F\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Psi||F_{\delta}|\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle+2\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Psi|\big|F_{\delta}-F\big|\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq 2\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|F_{\delta}|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Psi|^{2}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F_{\delta}-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+2C_{\Psi}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|F_{\delta}-F\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

Convergence of r.h.s of (
6.26
):  We also have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F^{2}_{\delta}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F^{2}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|F^{2}_{\delta}-F^{2}\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|F_{\delta}-F\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R+1}\big|F\big|^{2}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

Thus from these two estimates, (
6.26
) yields that

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot 2F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F^{2}({\bf y},{\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

By Defintion
6.4
,
F^{2}
has the
weak projection gradient
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F^{2}({\bf y},{\bf z})
in

{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}

and

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F^{2}({\bf y},{\bf z})=2F({\bf y},{\bf z})\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}).
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.10
.

Suppose that
F
satisfies the assumption in Lemma
6.10
and let
F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})=(J_{\delta}*F({\bf y},\cdot))({\bf z})=\int_{|{\bf u}\leq 1}J({\bf u})F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}){\rm d}{\bf u}
be defined in Lemma
6.11
.
Then

(6.27)

c_{R}:=\inf_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R+1}F({\bf y},{\bf z})>0,\quad\inf_{{\bf y}\in Y,|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R,0<\delta<1}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})\geq c_{R}.

Thus, as
\delta\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\Big|\sqrt{F_{\delta}}-\sqrt{F}\Big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{c_{R}}}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|F_{\delta}-F|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0,

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{F}}\Big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\leq\frac{1}{2c_{R}\sqrt{c_{R}}}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|F_{\delta}-F|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0.

Since
F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})
is smooth in
{\bf z}

and has positive lower bound on any bounded set, it follows that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bf z}\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}=\frac{\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}.

Thus for any
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})

(6.28)

\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}\,\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Convergence of l.h.s of (
6.28
):
Using (
6.24
) we have for all
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},0<\delta<1
and
{\bf y}\in Y\setminus Z_{\delta,{\bf z}}
that

\displaystyle\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}J({\bf u})\big|\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+C\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\bigg|\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf u}.

Then, thanks to Lemma
6.12
,

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}J({\bf u})\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|\Pi({\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf u}

\displaystyle+C\int_{|{\bf u}|\leq 1}\bigg(\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\bigg|\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u})}{|{\bf z}-\delta{\bf u}|}-\frac{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg){\rm d}{\bf u}\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

Next we have

\displaystyle\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\bigg|\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}-\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\frac{|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})|}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})|\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=:\frac{1}{2}A_{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}B_{\delta}

and from the lower bound (
6.27
) we have

\displaystyle A_{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{R}}}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}\leq R}|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})-\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

For
B_{\delta}
, we consider a decomposition: for any
0<M<\infty
,

(6.29)

\displaystyle B_{\delta}=\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F|1_{\{|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F|\leq M\}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{F}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle+\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})|1_{\{|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F|>M\}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{F}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle\leq M\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{F_{\delta}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{F}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{R}}}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F|1_{\{|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F|>M\}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

By definition of
F_{\delta}
, the first term in the righthand side of (
6.29
) tends to zero as
\delta\to 0^{+}
, while by the

L^{1}_{loc}
integrability of
|\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})|
, the second term in the righthand side of (
6.29
)
tends to zero as
M\to\infty
.
Thus by first letting
\delta\to 0^{+}
and then letting
M\to\infty
we
conclude
\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0^{+}}B_{\delta}=0
.

Thus

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\bigg|\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}-\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=0.

From this we deduce the convergence:

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\bigg|\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}}-\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}\bigg|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

Convergence of r.h.s of (
6.28
):  Since
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}\,\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}\,\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi}\int_{|{\bf y}|\leq R,|{\bf z}|\leq R}\big|\sqrt{F_{\delta}({\bf y},{\bf z})}-\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}\big|{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}\to 0\quad(\delta\to 0^{+}).

Letting
\delta\to 0^{+}
in (
6.28
) we finally obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}\,\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Thus
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}
exists and

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}=\frac{\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})}{2\sqrt{F({\bf y},{\bf z})}}.

∎

6.3.3.
Application to Boltzmann collision operator

We begin with an algebraic lemma.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
{\bf n}\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})=\{\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\,|\,\sigma\,\bot\,{\bf n}\,\},A\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{3\times 3}
, and
{\bf a},{\bf b}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}
. Then

(6.30)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\sigma^{\tau}A\sigma{\rm d}\sigma={\rm Tr}(A)-{\bf n}^{\tau}A{\bf n},

(6.31)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}({\bf a}\cdot\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma=\Pi({\bf n}){\bf a},\quad\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}({\bf a}\cdot\sigma)({\bf b}\cdot\sigma){\rm d}\sigma={\bf a}\cdot\Pi({\bf n}){\bf b}.

Proof.

Suppose

{\bf i},{\bf j}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
and
\{{\bf i},{\bf j},{\bf n}\}
is an orthonormal base of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
Then for the orthogonal matrix
T=({\bf i}\,\,{\bf j}\,\,{\bf n})^{\tau}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}

we have

T{\bf i}={\bf e}_{1}=(1,0,0)^{\tau},\,T{\bf j}={\bf e}_{2}=(0,1,0)^{\tau},\,T{\bf n}={\bf e}_{3}=(0,0,1)^{\tau}.

It is easy to see that

{\bf i}^{\tau}A{\bf i}+{\bf j}^{\tau}A{\bf j}+{\bf n}^{\tau}A{\bf n}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}{\bf e}_{i}^{\tau}TAT^{-1}{\bf e}_{i}={\rm Tr}(TAT^{-1})={\rm Tr}(A)
. Hence

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{1}({\bf z})}\sigma^{\tau}A\sigma{\rm d}\sigma=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\cos(\theta){\bf i}+\sin(\theta){\bf j})^{\tau}A(\cos(\theta){\bf i}+\sin(\theta){\bf j}){\rm d}\theta={\bf i}^{\tau}A{\bf i}+{\bf j}^{\tau}A{\bf j}={\rm Tr}(A)-{\bf n}^{\tau}A{\bf n}.

It is not difficult to check that

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}({\bf a}\cdot\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}({\bf a}\cdot(\cos(\theta){\bf i}+\sin(\theta){\bf j}))(\cos(\theta){\bf i}+\sin(\theta){\bf j}){\rm d}\theta=({\bf a}\cdot{\bf i}){\bf i}+({\bf a}\cdot{\bf j}){\bf j}={\bf a}-({\bf a}\cdot{\bf n}){\bf n}=\Pi({\bf n}){\bf a},

which yields that

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}({\bf a}\cdot\sigma)({\bf b}\cdot\sigma){\rm d}\sigma=\big(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}({\bf a}\cdot\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma\big)\cdot{\bf b}=\Pi({\bf n}){\bf a}\cdot{\bf b}={\bf a}\cdot\Pi({\bf n}){\bf b}.

∎

Since the Boltzmann collision operator involves integration on the unit sphere
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
, to apply the definition of
weak projection gradient
to the weak coupling limit, we need to introduce the
following space of test functions:

(6.32)

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\psi\in C_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})\,\,\Big|\,\,\psi({\bf y},\cdot,\cdot)\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}),\,\psi({\bf y},{\bf 0},\sigma)=0\,\,{\rm for\,\,all\,\,}{\bf y}\in Y,\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},

\displaystyle\sup_{({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\in Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},}|\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)|<\infty,\,\,\sup_{({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\in Y\times({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\frac{|\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)|}{|{\bf z}|}<\infty\bigg\}.

Here
\psi({\bf y},\cdot,\cdot)\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
can be understood as there is an open set
\Omega\supset{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
(it may depend on
{\bf y}
) such that
\psi({\bf y},\cdot,\cdot)\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times\Omega)
, and so

\sigma\mapsto\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)
is the usual gradient taken
in open set.

The following lemma is used to estimate bounds of difference quotients of functions
on the sphere.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
\psi\in C^{1}({{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1})
(which means that there is an open set
\Omega\supset{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}

such that
\psi\in C^{1}(\Omega)
). Then

|\psi(\sigma_{1})-\psi(\sigma_{2})|\leq 4\sqrt{2}\|\nabla\psi\|_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}}|\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|\qquad\forall\,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}

where
\|\nabla\psi\|_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}}=\max\limits_{\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}}|\nabla\psi(\sigma)|
.

Proof.

Denote
\|\nabla\psi\|=\|\nabla\psi\|_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}}
.
Take any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}
.

Case 1:

\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{2}\geq 0.
Consider

t\mapsto\gamma(t)={\bf a}(t)/|{\bf a}(t)|\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}
with

{\bf a}(t)=t\sigma_{1}+(1-t)\sigma_{2},t\in[0,1]
. We have

|{\bf a}(t)|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},{\bf a}^{\prime}(t)=\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2},
for all
t\in[0,1].

This gives

|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2|{\bf a}^{\prime}(t)|/|{\bf a}(t)|\leq 2\sqrt{2}|\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|
for all
t\in[0,1].
So we have for some
t\in(0,1)

|\psi(\sigma_{1})-\psi(\sigma_{2})|=|\psi\circ\gamma(1)-\psi\circ\gamma(0)|=|\nabla\psi(\sigma)\cdot\gamma^{\prime}(t)|\big|_{\sigma=\gamma(t)}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\nabla\psi\||\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|.

Case 2:

\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{2}<0.
In this case we have

|\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|\geq\sqrt{2}
and we can choose some
\sigma_{0}\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}
such that

\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{0}\geq 0,\sigma_{2}\cdot\sigma_{0}\geq 0
. [For instance
if
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}={\bf 0}
, then take

\sigma_{0}\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{n-1}
such that
\sigma_{0}\cdot\sigma_{1}=0
;
if
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}\neq{\bf 0}
, then choose

\sigma_{0}=(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})/|\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}|
.]
Applying Case 1 to
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{2}
respectively and
noting that
|\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}|,|\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{2}|\leq\sqrt{2}\leq|\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|

we obrain

|\psi(\sigma_{1})-\psi(\sigma_{2})|\leq|\psi(\sigma_{1})-\psi(\sigma_{0})|+|\psi(\sigma_{0})-\psi(\sigma_{2})|\leq 4\sqrt{2}\|\nabla\psi\||\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}|.

∎

Lemma 6.15
.

For any
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, let

\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma
. Then

\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma=\sigma^{\tau}\Psi^{\prime}_{{\bf z}}({\bf y},{\bf z})\sigma,\quad\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}\sigma=\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}).

Proof.

That

\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and the first two equalities are obvious.
Then applying Lemma
6.13
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{S}}^{1}({\bf z})}\sigma^{\tau}\Psi^{\prime}_{\bf z}({\bf y},{\bf z})\sigma{\rm d}\sigma={\rm Tr}(\Psi^{\prime}_{\bf z}({\bf y},{\bf z}))-{\bf n}^{\tau}\Psi^{\prime}_{\bf z}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\bf n}=\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})-{\bf n}^{\tau}\Psi^{\prime}_{\bf z}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\bf n},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}{\rm d}\sigma=2\frac{\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}

and so using (
6.14
) yields the third equality.
∎

Let
\nu
be a Borel measure on
Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
defined by

(6.33)

\nu(E)=\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}1_{E}({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y},\quad E\subset Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}

with
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
.
It is easily seen that the following equality

(6.34)

\int_{Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}g({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma){\rm d}\nu({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}g({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

holds for all
nonnegative Borel measurable or
\nu
- integrable functions
g
on
Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
.

The last lemma below shows that the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
can be characterized by
a vector-integration on the perpendicular circle
{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})
,
and plays an important role in the proof of Theorem
1.12
(From Eq.(MB) to Eq.(FPL)).

Lemma 6.16
.

A function
F\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
has the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
, if and only if there is a Borel measurable
function
D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)
on
Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
such that

D\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},{\rm d}\nu)
and it holds for all

\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
that

(6.35)

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Moreover, if
(\ref{6.34})
holds for such a function
D

(equivalently if
\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})
exists), then

\displaystyle\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma\quad{\rm a.e.}\quad on\quad Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},

\displaystyle D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma\qquad{\rm a.e.}\quad[\nu]\quad on\quad Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}.

Proof.

First of all we note that the function
D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)

(up to a set of
\nu
-measure zero) is uniquely determined by the identity
(
6.35
).

As before, we use smooth approximation. Let

j(t)=c\exp\big(\frac{-1}{1-t^{2}}\big)1_{\{|t|<1\}}
with
c>0

satisfy
\int_{{{\mathbb{R}}}}j(t){\rm d}t=1
, let

j_{\delta}(t)=\delta^{-1}j(\delta^{-1}t),\delta>0
, and let

\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)=\zeta(|{\bf z}|/\delta)
be the smooth function used in the proof of
Lemma
6.8
.
Suppose that
F
has the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})

in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
. Take
any
\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
. Note that by definition of

{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
there are no problems of integrability for all integrands below.
By continuity of
\sigma\mapsto\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma
on
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
,
it is easily shown that (with
{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|
)

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma{\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Thanks to the smooth cutoff
\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)
, the function

({\bf y},{\bf z})\mapsto\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma
belongs to
{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}).

Hence, using also (
6.19
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}{\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\big(\Pi({\bf z})j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\big){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}{\rm d}\sigma.

By elementary calculation we have (for
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\big(\Pi({\bf z})j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\big)=j_{\delta}^{\prime}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\frac{1-({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)^{2}}{|{\bf z}|}

\displaystyle+j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-({\bf n}\cdot\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)){\bf n}\cdot\sigma-2\frac{\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\sigma\cdot{\bf n}\Big)

and then we compute using (
2.2
) that

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\big(\Pi({\bf z})j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\big){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}j^{\prime}(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\psi\big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\big)}{|{\bf z}|}{\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}s

\displaystyle-\delta\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}s^{2}j^{\prime}(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\psi\big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\big)}{|{\bf z}|}{\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}j(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi\big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\big)\cdot\sigma{\rm d}\sigma\bigg)\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}{\rm d}s

\displaystyle-2\delta\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}sj(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\psi\Big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\bigg)}{|{\bf z}|}{\rm d}\sigma\Big){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=:I_{1,\delta}({\bf y,z})+I_{2,\delta}({\bf y,z})+I_{3,\delta}({\bf y,z})+I_{4,\delta}({\bf y,z}).

Estimate of
I_{1,\delta}({\bf y,z})
:  Using

\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}j^{\prime}(s){\rm d}s=0
, the first term becomes

\displaystyle I_{1,\delta}({\bf y,z})=\int_{-1/\delta}^{1/\delta}j^{\prime}(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\psi\Big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\Big)-\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{\delta|{\bf z}|}{\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}s.

For any
{\bf y}\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})
, and
s\in{{\mathbb{R}}}
,
we have

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{\psi\big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\big)-\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{\delta|{\bf z}|}=s\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}.

Also, applying Lemma
6.14
to
{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}\ni\sigma\mapsto\psi({\bf y,z},\sigma)
, there exists
0<C_{\psi}<\infty
such that for all
\delta>0

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in Y,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},\sigma\in{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\bigg|\frac{\psi\big({\bf y},{\bf z},{\bf n}\delta s+\sqrt{1-(\delta s)^{2}}\sigma\big)-\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{\delta|{\bf z}|}\bigg|\leq C_{\psi}|s|\quad\forall\,s\in[-1/\delta,1/\delta].

It follows from dominated convergence theorem and
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}sj^{\prime}(s){\rm d}s=-1
that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}I_{1,\delta}({\bf y,z})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}j^{\prime}(s)\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}s\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}{\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}s=-\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}{\rm d}\sigma.

Moreover we have

\sup\limits_{{\bf y}\in Y,\,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},\delta>0}|I_{1,\delta}({\bf y,z})|<\infty
.

Estimates of
I_{2,\delta}({\bf y,z})
,
I_{3,\delta}({\bf y,z})
,
I_{4,\delta}({\bf y,z})
:
It is easily seen that

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,\,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}}\big(|I_{2,\delta}({\bf y,z})|+|I_{4,\delta}({\bf y,z})|\big)\leq C_{\psi}\delta,\quad\forall\,\delta>0;

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf y}\in Y,\,{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\},\delta>0}|I_{3,\delta}({\bf y,z})|<\infty,\quad\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}I_{3,\delta}({\bf y,z})=\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma{\rm d}\sigma.

Thus it follows from dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Big(\Pi({\bf z})j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\Big){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\bigg(-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}+\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma\bigg){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\zeta_{\delta}(|{\bf z}|)F({\bf y},{\bf z})\bigg(\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}}\nabla_{{\bf z}}\cdot\Big(\Pi({\bf z})j_{\delta}({\bf n}\cdot\sigma)\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma\Big){\rm d}\sigma\bigg){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Collecting the above results we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma{\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

This shows that the function
D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma):=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma

satisfies (
6.35
).

Conversely, suppose that there is a Borel measurable
function
D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)
on
Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}
such that

D\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}},{\rm d}\nu)
and
(
6.35
) holds. Let

{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma,\quad{\bf n}={\bf z}/|{\bf z}|.

Then
{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}({\bf z})\,({\bf z}\neq{\bf 0})
and

{\bf D}\in L^{1}_{loc}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
.
For any
\Psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}},{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}})
, let
\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma
, then
\psi\in{\cal T}_{c}(Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}})
and by (
6.35
) and Lemma
6.15
we have

\displaystyle\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot{\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\Big(\nabla_{{\bf z}}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\cdot\sigma-\frac{\nabla_{\sigma}\psi({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)}{|{\bf z}|}\cdot{\bf n}\Big){\rm d}\sigma{\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}

\displaystyle=-\int_{Y\times{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\nabla_{\bf z}\cdot\Pi({\bf z})\Psi({\bf y},{\bf z}){\rm d}{\bf z}{\rm d}{\bf y}.

Thus, by definition,
F
has the weak projection gradient

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})

in
{\bf z}\in{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
and

\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})={\bf D}({\bf y},{\bf z})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma.

Finally using (
6.31
) we also have

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{{\mathbb{S}}}^{1}({\bf n})}\big(\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma\big)\sigma{\rm d}\sigma=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z}).

Since
D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)
is unique, it follows that

D({\bf y},{\bf z},\sigma)=\Pi({\bf z})\nabla_{{\bf z}}F({\bf y},{\bf z})\cdot\sigma
a.e.
[\nu]
on
Y\times{{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}.
This completes the proof.
∎
